Remark OA1.1

(Remainder of proof)
.

The proof for
Theorem

A.1
hinges on the key result in
Section

OA1.2
for
bounding
\xi_{3}
. Effectively, the argument first relates
\xi_{3}
to the corresponding
regret for the transformed parameters
\tau_{i}
(
OA1
):

\lVert\tau_{\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert^{2}.

To prove a bound for this object, we truncate to the event where
\overline{h}^{2}(f_{\hat{G}_{n},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})
is small and use the fact that, loosely speaking, the
\lVert\tau_{\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert^{2}
can be bounded by
\overline{h}^{2}(f_{\hat{G}_{n},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})
. For this argument to work, the key is that the event where

\overline{h}^{2}(f_{\hat{G}_{n},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})
is small has high probability, which is
shown in

OA1.1
. Lastly, to prove
OA1.1
, we need
to first establish that
\hat{G}_{n}
—estimated off
(\hat{Z}_{i},\hat{\nu}_{i})
—does not have
high likelihood suboptimality
\mathrm{Sub}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n})
. This is the most laborious part of the
proof (
SM1.1
).

\blacksquare

Lemma OA1.3
.

Under the assumptions of
Theorem

A.1
, in the proof of
Theorem

A.1
,

\xi_{1}\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}M_{n}^{2}(\log n)^{2}\Delta_{n}^{2}
.

Proof.

Note that, by an application of Taylor’s theorem,

\displaystyle\left|\hat{\theta}_{i,\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta}}-\hat{\theta}_{i,\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0}}\right|

\displaystyle=\sigma_{i}^{2}\left|\frac{f^{\prime}_{\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{i}}(\hat{Z}_{i})}{\hat{s}_{i}f_{\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{i}}(\hat{Z}_{i})}-\frac{f^{\prime}_{\hat{G}_{n},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}{s_{0i}f_{\hat{G}_{n},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}\right|

(
Equation

SM1.4
)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{i}^{2}\left|\left({\frac{\partial{\psi_{i}}}{\partial{m_{i}}}\bigg\rvert_{\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta}}-\frac{\partial{\psi_{i}}}{\partial{m_{i}}}\bigg\rvert_{\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0}}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\sigma_{i}^{2}\left|\frac{\partial{{}^{2}\psi_{i}}}{\partial{m_{i}\partial s_{i}}}\bigg\rvert_{\hat{G}_{n},\tilde{\eta}_{i}}(\hat{s}_{i}-s_{0i})+\frac{\partial{{}^{2}\psi_{i}}}{\partial{m_{i}^{2}}}\bigg\rvert_{\hat{G}_{n},\tilde{\eta}_{i}}(\hat{m}_{i}-m_{0i})\right|,

where we use
\tilde{\eta}_{i}
to denote some intermediate value lying on the line segment
between
\hat{\eta}_{i}
and
\eta_{0i}
. By
Lemma

SM1.11
, we can bound the
two derivative terms and obtain

\mathbbm{1}(A_{n})\left|\hat{\theta}_{i,\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta}}-\hat{\theta}_{i,\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0}}\right|\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}M_{n}(\log n)\Delta_{n}.

Hence, squaring both sides, we obtain
\xi_{1}\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}M_{n}^{2}(\log n)^{2}\Delta_{n}^{2}.

∎

Lemma OA1.4
.

Under the assumptions of
Theorem

A.1
, in the proof of
Theorem

A.1
,

\mathbb{E}\xi_{4}\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{n}.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\theta^{*}_{i,\rho_{n}}-\theta^{*}_{i})^{2}]

\displaystyle=s_{0i}^{2}\int\left({\nu_{i}^{2}\frac{f^{\prime}_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(z)}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(z)}}\right)^{2}\left({1-\frac{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}\vee\frac{\rho_{n}}{\nu_{i}}}}\right)^{2}f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(z)\,dz

(Cauchy–Schwarz)

\displaystyle\leq s_{0i}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left({\nu_{i}^{2}\frac{f^{\prime}_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z)}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z)}}\right)^{4}}\right]^{1/2}\cdot\operatorname{P}\left[{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z)<\rho_{n}/\nu_{i}}\right]^{1/2}

(Tweedie’s formula,
Jensen’s
inequality, and

Lemma

SM1.9
)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\mathbb{E}[(\tau-Z)^{4}]^{1/2}\cdot\rho_{n}^{1/3}\operatorname{Var}(Z)^{1/6}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{n}.

In particular, the third line follows since by Tweedie’s formula and Jensen’s inequality

\mathbb{E}\left[{\left({\nu_{i}^{2}\frac{f^{\prime}_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z)}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z)}}\right)^{4}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbb{E}_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}[\tau-Z\mid Z]^{4}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}[(\tau-Z)^{4}]\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}1.

Therefore,
\mathbb{E}[\xi_{4}]\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\frac{1}{n}.

∎

OA1.2.
Controlling
\xi_{3}

Lemma OA1.5
.

Under the assumptions of
Theorem

A.1
, in the proof of
Theorem

A.1
,

\mathbb{E}\xi_{3}\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}(\log n)^{3}\delta_{n}^{2}
, where
\delta_{n}
is defined in (
OA1.1
).

Proof.

Observe that
\left|\hat{\theta}_{i,\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\theta^{*}_{i,\rho_{n}}\right|=s_{0i}\left|\hat{\tau}_{i,\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau^{*}_{i,\rho_{n}}\right|

where
\hat{\tau}_{i,\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}
is the regularized posterior with prior

\hat{G}_{n}
at conditional moments
\eta_{0}
and
\tau^{*}_{i,\rho_{n}}=\hat{\tau}_{i,G_{0},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}
, where we recall (
OA1
).

Thus, we shall focus on controlling

\mathbbm{1}(A_{n})\lVert\hat{\tau}_{\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau^{*}_{\rho_{n}}\rVert^{2}.

Fix the rate function
\delta_{n}
in (
OA1.1
) and the constant

B_{\mathcal{H}}
in
OA1.1
(which in turn depends on

C^{*}_{\mathcal{H}}
in
SM1.1
). Let
B_{n}=\{\overline{h}(f_{\hat{G}_{n},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})<B_{\mathcal{H}}\delta_{n}\}
be the event of a small average squared Hellinger
distance. Let
G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}
be a finite set of prior
distributions (chosen to be a net of
\left\{{G:\overline{h}(f_{G,\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})\leq\delta_{n}}\right\}
in
some distance), and let
\tau^{(j)}_{\rho_{n}}
be the posterior mean vector corresponding
to prior
G_{j}
with conditional moments
\eta_{0}
and regularization
\rho_{n}
.

Now, note that, for any
j
,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}(A_{n})\lVert\hat{\tau}_{\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\hat{\tau}_{\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert\mathbbm{1}(A_{n}\cap B_{n}^{\mathrm{C}})+\mathbbm{1}(A_{n}\cap B_{n})\left({\lVert\hat{\tau}_{\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert-\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert}\right)_{+}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\left({\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert-\mathbb{E}[\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert]}\right)_{+}+\mathbb{E}[\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert].

Then

\frac{\mathbbm{1}(A_{n})}{n}\lVert\hat{\tau}_{\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau^{*}_{\rho_{n}}\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{4}{n}\left({\zeta_{1}^{2}+\zeta_{2}^{2}+\zeta_{3}^{2}+\zeta_{4}^{2}}\right)

where

(OA1.13)

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert\hat{\tau}_{\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau^{*}_{\rho_{n}}\rVert^{2}\mathbbm{1}\left({A_{n}\cap B_{n}^{\mathrm{C}}}\right)

(OA1.14)

\displaystyle\zeta_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left({\lVert\hat{\tau}_{\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau^{*}_{\rho_{n}}\rVert-\max_{j\in[N]}\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert}\right)_{+}^{2}\mathbbm{1}(A_{n}\cap B_{n})

(OA1.15)

\displaystyle\zeta_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle=\max_{j\in[N]}\left({\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert-\mathbb{E}\left[{\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert}\right]}\right)^{2}_{+}

(OA1.16)

\displaystyle\zeta_{4}^{2}

\displaystyle=\max_{j\in[N]}\left({\mathbb{E}\left[{\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert}\right]}\right)^{2}.

The decomposition
\zeta_{1}
through
\zeta_{4}
is exactly analogous to Section D.3 in the
supplementary materials to

Soloff et al. 2024h
and to the proof of Theorem 1 in
Jiang 2020h
. In
particular,
\zeta_{1}
is the gap on the “bad event” where the average squared Hellinger
distance is large, which is manageable since
\mathbbm{1}(A_{n}\cap B_{n}^{\mathrm{C}})
has small
probability by
OA1.1
.
\zeta_{2}
is the distance from the
posterior means at
\hat{G}_{n}
to the closest posterior mean generated from the net

G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}
;
\zeta_{2}
is small if we make the net
\left\{{G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}}\right\}
very fine.

\zeta_{3}
measures the
distance between
\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert
and its expectation;

\zeta_{3}
can be controlled by (i) a large-deviation inequality and (ii) controlling the
metric entropy of the net (
SM1.2
). Lastly,
\zeta_{4}
measures the
expected distance between
\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}
and
\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}
; it is small since

G_{j}
are fixed priors with small average squared Hellinger distance.

However, our argument for
\zeta_{3}
is slightly different and avoids an argument in

Jiang & Zhang 2009e
which appears to not apply in the heteroskedastic setting.
See
OA1.2
.

The subsequent subsections control
\zeta_{1}
through
\zeta_{4}
, and find that
\zeta_{4}\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}(\log n)^{3}\delta_{n}^{2}
is the dominating term.
∎

OA1.2.1.
Controlling
\zeta_{1}

First, we note that,

\left({\hat{\tau}_{i,\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau^{*}_{i,\rho_{n}}}\right)^{2}\mathbbm{1}(A_{n}\cap B_{n}^{\mathrm{C}})\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\log(1/\rho_{n})\mathbbm{1}(A_{n}\cap B_{n}^{\mathrm{C}})=\log n\mathbbm{1}(A_{n}\cap B_{n}^{\mathrm{C}}).

By
OA1.1
,
\operatorname{P}(A_{n}\cap B_{n}^{\mathrm{C}})\leq\left({\frac{\log\log n}{\log 2}+9}\right)\frac{1}{n}
, and hence
\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\zeta_{1}^{2}\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\frac{\log n\log\log n}{n}.

OA1.2.2.
Controlling
\zeta_{2}

Choose
G_{1},\ldots,G_{N}
to be a minimal
\omega
-covering of
\left\{{G:\overline{h}(f_{G,\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})\leq\delta_{n}}\right\}
under the pseudometric

d_{M_{n},\rho_{n}}(H_{1},H_{2})=\max_{i\in[n]}\sup_{z:|z|\leq M_{n}}\Bigg\lvert\frac{\nu_{i}^{2}f^{\prime}_{H_{1},\nu_{i}}(z)}{f_{H_{1},\nu_{i}}(z)\vee(\rho_{n}/\nu_{i})}-\frac{\nu_{i}^{2}f^{\prime}_{H_{2},\nu_{i}}(z)}{f_{H_{2},\nu_{i}}(z)\vee(\rho_{n}/\nu_{i})}\Bigg\rvert

where
N\leq N\left({\omega/2,\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}),d_{M_{n},\rho_{n}}}\right)
.
37
37

37

This is by the
monotonicity relation of covering numbers. See Exercise 4.2.10 in

Vershynin 2018h
.
We note that
(
OA1.2.2
) and
d_{m,\infty,M}
(
SM1.5
) are different only
by constant factors, in the sense that
d_{M_{n},\rho_{n}}(H_{1},H_{2})\asymp_{\mathcal{H}}d_{m,\infty,M}(H_{1},H_{2})
for all
H_{1},H_{2}
.
Therefore,
SM1.2
implies that

\log N\left({\frac{\delta\log(1/\delta)}{\rho_{n}}\sqrt{\log(1/\rho_{n})},\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}),d_{M_{n},\rho_{n}}}\right)\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\log(1/\delta)^{2}\max\left({1,\frac{M_{n}}{\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}}}\right).

Then,

(
(a-\max_{j}b_{j})_{+}\leq\min_{j}|a-b_{j}|
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\zeta_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbbm{1}(A_{n}\cap B_{n})}{n}\min_{j\in[N]}\left({\lVert\hat{\tau}_{\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau^{*}_{\rho_{n}}\rVert-\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert}\right)^{2}

(Triangle inequality :

\lvert\lVert a-b\rVert-\lVert b-c\rVert\rvert\leq\lVert a-c\rVert
)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{1}(A_{n}\cap B_{n})\frac{1}{n}\min_{j\in[N]}\lVert\hat{\tau}_{\hat{G}_{n},\eta_{0},\rho_{n}}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}(A_{n}\cap B_{n})\min_{j\in[N]}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}\left({|Z_{i}|\leq M_{n}}\right)\left({\frac{\nu_{i}^{2}f^{\prime}_{\hat{G}_{n},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}{f_{\hat{G}_{n},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})\vee(\rho_{n}/\nu_{i})}-\frac{\nu_{i}^{2}f^{\prime}_{G_{j},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{j},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})\vee(\rho_{n}/\nu_{i})}}\right)^{2}

(Reparametrize

\omega=2\delta\log(1/\delta)\rho_{n}^{-1}\sqrt{\log(1/\rho_{n})}
)

\displaystyle\leq\omega^{2}\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\frac{\delta^{2}\log(1/\delta)^{2}}{\rho_{n}^{2}}\log(1/\rho_{n}).

OA1.2.3.
Controlling
\zeta_{3}

We first observe that
V_{ij}\equiv|\tau_{i,\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{i,\rho_{n}}^{*}|\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{\log n}
, by

Lemma

SM1.8
. Let
V_{j}=(V_{1j},\ldots,V_{nj})^{\prime}
, we have that
\zeta_{3}=\max_{j}(\lVert V_{j}\rVert-\mathbb{E}\lVert V_{j}\rVert)_{+}.
Let
K_{n}=C_{\mathcal{H}}\sqrt{\log n}\geq\max_{ij}|V_{ij}|
. Since
G_{j},G_{0}
are both fixed,
V_{1j},\ldots,V_{nj}
are mutually independent, and
V_{ij}/K_{n}\in[0,1]
. Then, observe that by
Lemma

OA1.8
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{P}\left({\lVert V_{j}\rVert>\mathbb{E}[\lVert V_{j}\rVert]+u}\right)=\operatorname{P}\left({\left\lVert\frac{V_{j}}{K_{n}}\right\rVert\geq\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\frac{V_{j}}{K_{n}}\right\rVert+\frac{u}{K_{n}}}\right)\leq\exp\left({-\frac{u^{2}}{2K_{n}^{2}}}\right).

By union bound,

\operatorname{P}\left({\zeta_{3}^{2}>x}\right)\leq N\exp\left({-\frac{x}{2K_{n}^{2}}}\right).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\zeta_{3}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{P}(\zeta_{3}^{2}>x)\,dx\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\min\left({1,N\exp\left({-\frac{x}{2K_{n}^{2}}}\right)}\right)\,dx

\displaystyle=2K_{n}^{2}\log N+\int_{2K_{n}^{2}\log N}^{\infty}N\exp\left({-\frac{x}{2K_{n}^{2}}}\right)\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\log n\log N.

Now, if we take
\delta=\rho_{n}/n
, then
\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\zeta_{2}^{2}+\zeta_{3}^{2}]\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\frac{(\log n)^{5/2}M_{n}}{n}
.

Remark OA1.2
.

For the analogous term in the homoskedastic setting,
Jiang & Zhang 2009e
observe that
\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert
is a Lipschitz function of the noise component
Z_{i}-\tau_{i}
. As a result, a Gaussian
isoperimetric inequality
(Theorem 5.6 in
Boucheron et al. 2013b
) bounds

\operatorname{P}\left({\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert\geq\mathbb{E}\left[{\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert\mid\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}}\right]+x}\right),

independently of
n
—a fact used in Proposition 4 of
Jiang & Zhang 2009e
. Note that the concentration of
\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert
is towards its conditional mean

\mathbb{E}\left[{\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert\mid\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}}\right].

In the homoskedastic setting where
\nu_{i}=\nu
,

\mathbb{E}\left[{\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert\mid\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{G_{0,n}}\left[{\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert}\right]

where
G_{0,n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\delta_{\tau_{i}}
is the empirical distribution of
the
\tau
’s. However, (
OA1.2
) no longer holds in the heteroskedastic
setting, and to adapt this argument, we need to additionally control the difference
between
\mathbb{E}\left[{\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert\mid\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}}\right]
and
\mathbb{E}\left[{\lVert\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}\rVert}\right]
. The argument in
Jiang 2020h
(p.2289) appears to use the
Gaussian concentration of Lipschitz functions argument without this additional step.
Instead, we establish control of
\zeta_{3}
by observing that entries of
\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{\rho_{n}}^{*}
are bounded and applying the convex Lipschitz concentration
inequality.

\blacksquare

OA1.2.4.
Controlling
\zeta_{4}

Consider a change of variables where we let
w_{i}=z/\nu_{i}
and
\lambda_{i}=\tau/\nu_{i}
. Let
G_{(i)}
be the distribution of
\lambda_{i}
under
G
, where

G_{(i)}(d\lambda)=G(d\tau).
Then

f_{G,\nu_{i}}(z)=\int\frac{1}{\nu_{i}}\varphi\left({w_{i}-\lambda_{i}}\right)G(d\tau)=\frac{1}{\nu_{i}}\int\varphi\left({w_{i}-\lambda_{i}}\right)G_{(i)}(d\lambda_{i})=\frac{1}{\nu_{i}}f_{G_{(i)},1}(w_{i})

and
f_{G,\nu_{i}}^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{\nu_{i}^{2}}f^{\prime}_{G_{(i)},1}(w_{i})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{(\tau_{i,\rho_{n}}^{(j)}-\tau_{i,\rho_{n}}^{*})^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\nu_{i}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left({\frac{f^{\prime}_{G_{j,(i)},1}(w_{i})}{f_{G_{j,(i)},1}(w_{i})\vee\rho_{n}}-\frac{f^{\prime}_{G_{0,(i)},1}(w_{i})}{f_{G_{0,(i)},1}(w_{i})\vee\rho_{n}}}\right)^{2}}\right]

(
Lemma

OA1.9
)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\max\left({(\log 1/\rho_{n})^{3},\lvert\log h(f_{G_{j,(i)},1},f_{G_{0,(i)},1})\rvert}\right)h^{2}(f_{G_{j,(i)},1},f_{G_{0,(i)},1})

(Hellinger distance is invariant to
change-of-variables)

\displaystyle=\max\left({(\log 1/\rho_{n})^{3},\lvert\log h(f_{G_{j},\nu_{i}},f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}})\rvert}\right)h^{2}(f_{G_{j},\nu_{i}},f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}})

Let
h_{i}=h(f_{G_{j},\nu_{i}},f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\zeta_{4}^{2}]

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\frac{(\log n)^{3}}{n}\sum_{i:\lvert\log h_{i}\rvert\leq(\log 1/\rho_{n})^{3}}h_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i:\lvert\log h_{i}\rvert>(\log 1/\rho_{n})^{3}}\lvert\log h_{i}\rvert h_{i}^{2}

(
x\lvert\log x\rvert\leq e^{-1}
for
x\in[0,1]
)

\displaystyle\leq(\log n)^{3}\overline{h}^{2}(f_{G_{j},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i:\lvert\log h_{i}\rvert>(\log 1/\rho_{n})^{3}}\frac{1}{e}h_{i}

Note that

\lvert\log h_{i}\rvert>(\log 1/\rho_{n})^{3}\implies h_{i}<\exp\left({-\log(1/\rho_{n})^{3}}\right)<\rho_{n}^{(\log 1/\rho_{n})^{2}}\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\rho_{n}^{3}\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}n^{-1}.

Therefore the first term dominates, and thus
\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\zeta_{4}^{2}]\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}(\log n)^{3}\delta_{n}^{2}.

OA1.3.
Auxiliary lemmas

Lemma OA1.6
.

Let
\hat{\theta}_{i,\hat{G},\hat{\eta}}
be the posterior mean at prior
\hat{G}
and
conditional moments estimate at
\hat{\eta}
. Let
\theta_{i}^{*}=\hat{\theta}_{i,G_{0},\eta_{0}}

be the oracle posterior mean. Assume that
\hat{G}
is supported within
[-\overline{M}_{n},\overline{M}_{n}]
where
\overline{M}_{n}=\max_{i}|\hat{Z}_{i}(\hat{\eta})\vee 1|
. Recall that
\lVert\hat{\eta}-\eta_{0}\rVert_{\infty}=\max(\lVert\hat{m}-m_{0}\rVert_{\infty},\lVert\hat{s}-s_{0}\rVert_{\infty})
. Suppose

(1)

\lVert\hat{\eta}-\eta_{0}\rVert_{\infty}\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}1
;

(2)

2
and
3
holds;

(3)

\hat{s}\gtrsim_{\mathcal{H}}s_{\ell n}
for some fixed sequence
s_{\ell n}>0
.

Then, letting
\overline{Z}_{n}=\max_{i}|Z_{i}|\vee 1
,

\left|\hat{\theta}_{i,\hat{G},\hat{\eta}}-\theta_{i}^{*}\right|\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\overline{s}_{\ell n}^{-1}\overline{Z}_{n}.

Moreover, the assumptions are satisfied by

1
,
4
,
2
, and
3

with
s_{\ell n}=s_{0\ell}\asymp 1
.

Proof.

Observe that

\displaystyle\left|\hat{\theta}_{i,\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta}}-\hat{\theta}_{i,G_{0},\eta_{0}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\hat{s}_{i}\frac{\hat{\nu}_{i}^{2}f^{\prime}_{\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{i}}(\hat{Z}_{i})}{f_{\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\nu}_{i}}(\hat{Z}_{i})}-s_{0i}\frac{\nu_{i}^{2}f^{\prime}_{G_{0}\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\overline{M}_{n}+\overline{Z}_{n}\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}\max_{i}\max(|\hat{Z}_{i}|,|Z_{i}|,1)

by the boundedness of
\hat{G}_{n}
and

Lemma

SM1.14
.
Note that
|\hat{Z}_{i}(\hat{\eta})|=\left|\frac{s_{0i}}{\hat{s}_{i}}Z_{i}+\frac{m_{0i}-\hat{m}_{i}}{\hat{s}_{i}}\right|\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}s_{\ell n}^{-1}\max(|Z_{i}|,1)=s_{\ell n}^{-1}\overline{Z}_{n}.
Therefore,
\lvert\hat{\theta}_{i,\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta}}-\hat{\theta}_{i,G_{0},\eta_{0}}\rvert\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}s_{\ell n}^{-1}\overline{Z}_{n}
.
∎

Lemma OA1.7
.

Let
\overline{Z}_{n}=\max_{i}|Z_{i}|\vee 1
. Under
2
, for
t>1

\operatorname{P}(\overline{Z}_{n}>t)\leq n\exp\left({-C_{A_{0},\alpha,\nu_{u}}t^{\alpha}}\right)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbb{E}[\overline{Z}_{n}^{p}]\lesssim_{p,\mathcal{H}}(\log n)^{p/\alpha}.

Moreover, if
M_{n}=(C_{\mathcal{H}}+1)(C_{2,\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\log n)^{1/\alpha}
as
in
(
OA1
), then for all sufficiently large choices of
C_{\mathcal{H}}
,
\operatorname{P}(\overline{Z}_{n}>M_{n})\leq n^{-2}.

Proof.

The first claim is immediate under
Lemma

SM1.12
and a union bound. The second
claim follows from the observation that

\mathbb{E}[\max_{i}(|Z_{i}|\vee 1)^{p}]\leq\left({\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[(|Z_{i}|\vee 1)^{pc}]}\right)^{1/c}\leq n^{1/c}C_{\mathcal{H}}^{p}(pc)^{p/\alpha}.

where the last inequality follows from simultaneous moment control. Choose
c=\log n

with
n^{1/\log n}=e
to finish the proof. For the “moreover” part, we have that

\operatorname{P}(Z_{n}>M_{n})\leq\exp\left({\log n-C_{A_{0},\alpha,\nu_{u}}(C_{\mathcal{H}}+1)^{\alpha}C_{2,\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\log n}\right)

and it suffices to choose
C_{\mathcal{H}}
such that
(C_{\mathcal{H}}+1)^{\alpha}>\frac{3C_{2,\mathcal{H}}}{C_{A_{0},\alpha,\nu_{u}}}
so that
\operatorname{P}(Z_{n}>M_{n})\leq e^{-2\log n}=n^{-2}
.
∎

Lemma OA1.8
.

Let
W=(W_{1},\ldots,W_{n})
be a vector containing independent entries, where
W_{i}\in[0,1]
. Let
\lVert\cdot\rVert
be the Euclidean norm. Then, for all
t>0

\operatorname{P}\left[{\lVert W\rVert>\mathbb{E}\lVert W\rVert+t}\right]\leq e^{-t^{2}/2}.

Proof.

We wish to use Theorem 6.10 of
Boucheron et al. 2013b
, which is a
dimension-free concentration inequality for convex Lipschitz functions of bounded random
variables. To do so, we observe that
w\mapsto\lVert w\rVert
is Lipschitz with respect to

\lVert\cdot\rVert
, since

\lVert w+a\rVert\leq\lVert w\rVert+\lVert a\rVert\hskip 10.00002pt\lVert w\rVert=\lVert w+a-a\rVert\leq\lVert w+a\rVert+\lVert a\rVert\implies|\lVert w+a\rVert-\lVert w\rVert|\leq\lVert a\rVert.

Moreover, trivially
\lVert\lambda w+(1-\lambda)v\rVert\leq\lambda\lVert w\rVert+(1-\lambda)\lVert v\rVert
for
\lambda\in[0,1]
, and hence
w\mapsto\lVert w\rVert
is convex. Convexity
implies the convexity required in Theorem 6.10 of
Boucheron et al. 2013b
. This
checks all conditions and the claim follows by applying Theorem 6.10 of

Boucheron et al. 2013b
.
∎

Lemma OA1.9
.

Let
f_{H}=f_{H,1}
. Then, for
0<\rho_{n}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi e^{2}}}
,

\displaystyle\int\left[{\frac{f^{\prime}_{H_{1}}(x)}{f_{H_{1}}(x)\vee\rho_{n}}-\frac{f^{\prime}_{H_{0}}(x)}{f_{H_{0}}(x)\vee\rho_{n}}}\right]^{2}f_{H_{0}}(x)\,dx\lesssim\left({(\log 1/\rho_{n})^{3}\vee\lvert\log h\left({f_{H_{1}},f_{H_{0}}}\right)\rvert}\right)h^{2}\left({f_{H_{1}},f_{H_{0}}}\right)

where we define the right-hand side to be zero if
H_{1}=H_{0}
.

Proof.

This claim is an intermediate step of Theorem 3 of
Jiang & Zhang 2009e
. In (3.10) in

Jiang & Zhang 2009e
, the left-hand side of this claim is defined as
r^{2}(f_{H_{1}},\rho_{n})
. Their subsequent calculation, which involves Lemma 1 of
Jiang & Zhang 2009e
,
proceeds to bound

r(f_{H_{1}},\rho_{n})\leq 2\sqrt{2}eh(f_{H_{1}},f_{H_{0}})\max\left({\varphi_{+}^{3}(\rho_{n}),\sqrt{2}a}\right)+2\varphi_{+}(\rho_{n})\sqrt{2}h(f_{H_{1}},f_{H_{0}}),

for
a^{2}=\max\left({\varphi_{+}^{2}(\rho_{n})+1,|\log h^{2}\left({f_{H_{1}},f_{H_{0}}}\right)|}\right)
in their paper. Collecting
the powers on
h,\log h
, squaring, and using
\varphi_{+}(\rho_{n})\lesssim\sqrt{\log(1/\rho_{n})}
proves the claim.
∎

Part II
Additional discussions and empirical results

Appendix OA2
Additional discussions

OA2.1.
Correlated
Y_{i}
and correlated
\theta_{i}

The assumptions (
1
) and (
2.1
)
imply that
(Y_{i},\theta_{i},\sigma_{i})
are i.i.d. across
i
. In general, we may consider
a joint distribution on
\theta_{1:n}\mid\Sigma
, conditional on which the estimates may
also be correlated
Y_{1:n}\mid\theta_{1:n}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta_{1:n},\Sigma)

(see, among others,
Bonhomme & Denis 2024e
;
Müller & Watson 2022b
, for settings in which the non-independence appears natural)
.
In
principle, given a flexible model for the distribution
\theta_{1:n}\mid\Sigma
, we
would estimate this model from the data
(Y_{1:n},\Sigma)
, and likewise compute
an estimated posterior. In particular, if
\theta_{1:n}\mid\Sigma
is described by
(
2.4
) but
Y_{1:n}
may be correlated conditional on
\theta_{1:n},\Sigma
, then
Items

close–step 1

and

close–step 2

continue to estimate
\theta_{1:n}\mid\Sigma
, and one can adapt
Item

close–step 3

accordingly to exploit the correlation
between
Y_{i}
’s.

Modeling the joint distribution of
(Y_{1:n},\theta_{1:n},\Sigma)
may be difficult.
Interestingly, modeling only the marginal distributions of each coordinate
(Y_{i},\theta_{i},\sigma_{i})
—as we have done—turns out to be sufficient for optimal
decision-making, if we restrict the class of decision rules.
For a compound decision
problem, where
L(\bm{\delta},\theta_{1:n})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\delta_{i},\theta_{i})
, consider restricting to
separable
decision rules
\delta_{i}=\delta_{i}(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})
, where we make decisions about
\theta_{i}
using only
(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})
. In that case, the best decision
\delta_{i}(\cdot,\cdot)
minimizes the
posterior risk
\mathbb{E}[\ell(\delta(Y_{i},\sigma_{i}),\theta_{i})\mid Y_{i},\sigma_{i}]
. This result provides some reassurance that ignoring the
dependence across coordinates is inefficient but may not be harmful, since naive
decisions are often separable, and the optimal separable rule would dominate it.
Restricting to separable decision rules can also be motivated by practical or fairness
considerations: For instance, it may be unfair to base human resource decisions on a
given teacher on the value-added estimate of another.

We formalize the above paragraph in
Lemma

OA2.1
. Suppose
(Y_{1:n},\theta_{1:n},\Sigma)
follow some joint distribution
Q_{0}
under which

Y_{1:n}\mid\theta_{1:n},\Sigma\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta_{1:n},\Sigma)
. We observe
(Y_{1:n},\Sigma)
. Consider a compound decision problem with a separable decision rule,
such that

L(\bm{\delta},\theta_{1:n})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\delta_{i}(Y_{i},\sigma_{i}),\theta_{i}).

Let
\sigma_{i}^{2}
denote the
i
th
diagonal element of
\Sigma
.

Lemma OA2.1
.

Let
(Y_{i},\theta_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})\sim H_{0i}
under
Q_{0}
. Assume
Q_{0}
is such that the
posterior mean

\mathbb{E}_{H_{0i}}\left[{\ell(a,\theta_{i})\mid Y_{i},\sigma_{i}}\right]
exists almost surely.
Consider the decision rule
\bm{\delta}
that minimizes posterior risk under
\theta_{i}\mid Y_{i},\sigma_{i}
over the action space

\delta_{i}(y,\sigma)\in\argmin_{a}\mathbb{E}_{H_{0i}}\left[{\ell(a,\theta_{i})\mid Y_{i}=y,\sigma_{i}=\sigma}\right].

Then such a decision is optimal for Bayes risk under
Q_{0}
:

\mathbb{E}_{Q_{0}}[L(\bm{\delta},\theta_{1:n})]=\min_{\tilde{\bm{\delta}}\text{ separable}}\mathbb{E}_{Q_{0}}[L(\tilde{\bm{\delta}},\theta_{1:n})].

Proof.

By definition, for any separable
\tilde{\bm{\delta}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Q_{0}}\left[{\ell(\delta_{i}(Y_{i},\sigma_{i}),\theta_{i})}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{H_{0i}}\left[{\ell(\delta_{i}(Y_{i},\sigma_{i}),\theta_{i})}\right]

(Law of iterated expectations)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbb{E}\left[{\ell(\delta_{i}(Y_{i},\sigma_{i}),\theta_{i})\mid Y_{i},\sigma_{i}}\right]}\right]

(
\delta_{i}
is Bayes rule)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbb{E}\left[{\ell(\tilde{\delta}_{i}(Y_{i},\sigma_{i}),\theta_{i})\mid Y_{i},\sigma_{i}}\right]}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{Q_{0}}\left[{\ell(\tilde{\delta}_{i}(Y_{i},\sigma_{i}),\theta_{i})}\right].

Thus by linearity of expectation,

\mathbb{E}_{Q_{0}}[L(\bm{\delta},\theta_{1:n})]\leq\mathbb{E}_{Q_{0}}[L(\tilde{\bm{\delta}},\theta_{1:n})].\qed

OA2.2.
Alternatives to
close

Let us turn to a few specific alternative methods that consider failure of precision
independence. We argue that they do not provide a free-lunch improvement over our
assumptions.

Alternative 1

(Working with
t
-ratios)
.

We may consider normalizing
\sigma_{i}
away by working with

t
-ratios
T_{i}\equiv\frac{Y_{i}}{\sigma_{i}}\mid(\sigma_{i},\theta_{i})\sim\mathcal{N}\left({\theta_{i}/\sigma_{i},1}\right).
The resulting problem is homoskedastic by construction. It is
natural to consider performing empirical Bayes shrinkage assuming that

\frac{\theta_{i}}{\sigma_{i}}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}H_{0}
, and use, say,
\sigma_{i}\mathbf{E}_{\hat{H}_{n}}\left[{\frac{\theta_{i}}{\sigma_{i}}\mid T_{i}}\right]
as an estimator for the posterior mean of
\theta_{i}

(
Jiang & Zhang 2010e
)
. However, without imposing
\theta_{i}/\sigma_{i}\indep\sigma_{i}
(which we discuss in
2
), such an approach approximates
the optimal decision rule within a restricted class on a different objective.

Let us restrict decision rules to those of the form
\delta_{i,\text{t-stat}}(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})=\sigma_{i}h(Y_{i}/\sigma_{i})
. The oracle Bayes choice of
h
is
h^{\star}(T_{i})=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[{\sigma_{i}\theta_{i}\mid T_{i}}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{i}^{2}\mid T_{i}]}
. However,
h^{\star}
is not the posterior mean of

\theta_{i}/\sigma_{i}
given the
t
-ratio
T_{i}
, unless
\sigma_{i}^{2}\indep\theta_{i}/\sigma_{i}
.
On the other hand, the loss function that does rationalize the posterior mean
h(T_{i})=\mathbb{E}[\theta_{i}/\sigma_{i}\mid T_{i}]
is the precision-weighted compound loss
L(\bm{\delta},\theta_{1:n})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}^{-2}(\delta_{i}-\theta_{i})^{2}
. Thus, rescaling posterior means on
t
-ratios
achieves optimality for a weighted objective among a restricted class of decision rules

\delta_{i,\text{t-stat}}
.

\blacksquare

Alternative 2

(Variance-stabilizing transforms)
.

Second, we may consider a variance-stabilizing transform when the underlying micro-data are
Bernoulli and
\theta_{i}
is a Bernoulli mean

(
Efron & Morris 1975e
;
Brown 2008e
)
. Specifically, we rely on the asymptotic
approximation

\sqrt{n_{i}}(Y_{i}-\theta_{i})\underset{n_{i}\to\infty}{\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,\theta_{i}(1-\theta_{i})).

A variance-stabilizing transform can disentangle the dependence: Let

W_{i}=2\arcsin(\sqrt{Y_{i}})
and
\omega_{i}=2\arcsin(\sqrt{\theta_{i}})
, and, by the delta
method,

\sqrt{n_{i}}\left({W_{i}-\omega_{i}}\right)\underset{n_{i}\to\infty}{\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,1).\hskip 10.00002pt\text{
Thus, approximately, }W_{i}\mid\omega_{i},n_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left({\omega_{i},\frac{1}{n_{i}}}\right).

One might consider an empirical Bayes approach on the resulting
W_{i}
. Note that
W_{i}

may still violate precision independence, since
\omega_{i}
may not be independent of

n_{i}
.
Moreover, squared error loss on estimating
\omega_{i}=2\arcsin(\sqrt{\theta_{i}})
is
different from squared error loss on estimating
\theta_{i}
. We do not know of any
guarantees for the loss function on
\theta_{i}
,
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\delta_{i}-\sin^{2}(\omega_{i}/2))^{2}
, when we perform empirical Bayes analysis on

\omega_{i}
.

\blacksquare

Alternative 3

(Treating the standard error as estimated)
.

Lastly, if the researcher has access to micro-data,

Gu & Koenker 2017b
and
Fu et al. 2020b
propose empirical Bayes strategies
that treat
\sigma_{i}
as noisy as well, in which we know the likelihood of
(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})
. This approach allows for dependence between
\theta_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}
but
assumes independence between
(\theta_{i},\sigma_{i})
and some other known
parameter. To describe their model, we introduce more notation. Let
Y_{ij},j=1,\ldots,n_{i},
denote the micro-data for population
i
, where, for each
i
, we
are interested in the mean of
Y_{ij}
. Let
Y_{i}
denote their sample mean and
S_{i}^{2}

denote their sample variance, where
\sigma_{i}^{2}=S_{i}^{2}/n_{i}
. Let
\sigma_{i0}^{2}
denote
the true variance of observations from population
i
.

Both papers work under Gaussian assumptions on the micro-data. This parametric
assumption
38
38

38

The parametric restriction on the micro-data
Y_{ij}
can be relaxed
by appealing to the asymptotic distribution of
(Y_{i},S_{i}^{2})
—resulting in the Gaussian
likelihood
(Y_{i},S_{i}^{2})\mid\bm{\theta}_{i},\Sigma_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\theta}_{i},\Sigma_{i})
. In
general, however,
\Sigma_{i}
also depends on
n_{i}
and higher moments of
Y_{ij}
, which
again may not be independent of
\bm{\theta}_{i}
.
on the micro-data—which is stronger than
we require—implies that
Y_{i}\indep S_{i}^{2}\mid(\sigma_{i0},\theta_{i},n_{i})
with
marginal distributions:

\displaystyle Y_{i}\mid\sigma_{i0},\theta_{i},n_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left({\theta_{i},\frac{\sigma_{i0}^{2}}{n_{i}}}\right)\hskip 20.00003ptS_{i}^{2}\mid\sigma_{i0},\theta_{i},n_{i}\sim\operatorname{Gamma}\left({\frac{n_{i}-1}{2},\frac{1}{2\sigma_{i0}^{2}}}\right).

They then propose empirical Bayes methods treating
\mathbf{Y}_{i}\equiv(Y_{i},S_{i}^{2})

as noisy estimates for parameters
\bm{\theta}_{i}\equiv(\theta_{i},\sigma_{i0}^{2})
. This
formulation allows
\bm{\theta}_{i}
to have a flexible distribution, and thus allows for
dependence between
\theta_{i}
and
\sigma_{i0}^{2}
. However, since the known sample size

n_{i}
enters the likelihood of
\mathbf{Y}_{i}
, this approach still assumes that

n_{i}\indep\bm{\theta}_{i}
.

\blacksquare

Alternative 4

(
f
-modeling)
.

A final alternative is to exploit Tweedie’s formula
(
Efron 2022e
)
, which
implies that an estimate of the conditional distribution
Y_{i}\mid\sigma_{i}
is all one needs for computing the posterior means

(
Brown & Greenshtein 2009b
;
Liu et al. 2020e
;
Luo et al. 2023b
)
. However, conditional
density estimation is a challenging problem, and most available methods do not exploit
the restriction that
Y_{i}\mid\sigma_{i}
is a Gaussian convolution.

\blacksquare

This discussion is not to say that
close
is necessarily preferable to these alternatives.
It highlights that the possible dependence between
\theta_{i}
and
\sigma_{i}
cannot be
easily resolved. Existing alternatives compromise
on optimality, use a different loss function, or implicitly assume
\theta_{i}
is
independent from components of
\sigma_{i}^{2}
(e.g.,
n_{i}
). Of course, depending on the
empirical context, these may well be reasonable features.

OA2.3.
Transformation-based rationalization of the location-scale assumption
(
2.4
)

The following lemma shows that, essentially, only affine transforms preserve exponential
family structure on
Y_{i}
. Exponential family structure is important since
generalizations of Tweedie’s formula holds for such distributions
(
Efron 2011e
)
, and thus they connect posterior means to the marginal distribution of
the data. If some affine transform yields precision independence—so as to allow for
methods
that assume precision independence—then the location-scale assumption (
2.4
) must hold.

Lemma OA2.2
.

Let
Y_{i}\mid\theta_{i},\sigma_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta_{i},\sigma_{i}^{2})
and
(\theta_{i},\sigma_{i})
drawn i.i.d. Consider
h(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})
such that
h(\cdot,\sigma_{i})
is differentiable and strictly increasing. Let

Z_{i}=h(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})
. Then the distribution of
Z_{i}
, parametrized by
\theta_{i}
,
is an exponential family

p(z\mid\theta_{i},\sigma_{i})=\exp\left({\eta(\theta_{i},\sigma_{i})z+A(\eta(\theta_{i},\sigma_{i}),\sigma_{i})}\right)f_{0}(z,\sigma_{i})

only if
h(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})=a(\sigma_{i})+b(\sigma_{i})Y_{i}
.

Moreover, suppose
\theta_{i}\mid\sigma_{i}
has finite first and second moments. When
(
OA2.2
) holds for some
h(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})=a(\sigma_{i})+b(\sigma_{i})Y_{i}
,
the distribution of
Z_{i}=h(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})
is of the form

Z_{i}\mid\theta_{i},\sigma_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\tau(\theta_{i},\sigma_{i}),\nu^{2}(\sigma_{i})).

The distribution of
\tau(\theta_{i},\sigma_{i})\mid\sigma_{i}
does not depend on
\sigma_{i}

only if (
2.4
) holds.

Proof.

The first part of the statement follows immediately from
Lemma

OA2.3
. For the
second part, it is easy to see that
\tau_{i}\equiv\tau(\theta_{i},\sigma_{i})=\theta_{i}b(\sigma_{i})+a(\sigma_{i})
.
If
\tau(\theta_{i},\sigma_{i})\mid\sigma_{i}
does not depend on
\sigma_{i}
, then
\mathbb{E}[\tau\mid\sigma_{i}],\operatorname{Var}(\tau\mid\sigma_{i})

are constant in
\sigma_{i}
. This means that
b(\sigma_{i})=1/\sqrt{\operatorname{Var}(\theta_{i}\mid\sigma_{i})}
and
a(\sigma_{i})=-\mathbb{E}[\theta_{i}\mid\sigma_{i}]b(\sigma_{i})
. Rearrange, we
have

\theta_{i}=s_{0}(\sigma_{i})\tau_{i}+m_{0}(\sigma_{i})\hskip 10.00002pt\tau_{i}\mid\sigma_{i}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}G_{0},

which is the condition (
2.4
).
∎

Lemma OA2.3
.

Consider a family of densities
\left\{{\mathcal{N}(\theta,\sigma^{2}):\theta\in\mathbb{R}}\right\}
. Let
h(\cdot):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a strictly increasing
and
differentiable function. Let
Z=h(Y)
where
Y\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta,\sigma^{2})
. The corresponding family of densities for
Z
is an exponential
family:

p_{Z}(z)=f_{0}(z;\sigma)\exp\left({z\eta(\theta,\sigma)+A(\eta;\sigma)}\right)

for some canonical parameter
\eta(\theta;\sigma)

if and only if
h(\cdot)
is affine.

Proof.

The “if” part is immediate. We will focus on the “only if” part. By the change of
variables formula, the density of
Z
is

p_{Z}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma}\exp\left({-\frac{1}{2}\frac{h^{-1}(z)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+h^{-1}(z)\frac{\theta}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{\theta^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}\right)\frac{dh^{-1}(z)}{dz}.

The log-likelihood ratio of this family is

\log\left[{p_{Z}(z\mid\theta_{1})/p_{Z}(z\mid\theta_{2})}\right]={h^{-1}(z)\frac{\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(\theta_{1}^{2}-\theta_{2}^{2})}.

For an exponential family (
OA2.3
), the log-likelihood ratio is
z\left({\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}}\right)+A(\eta_{1};\sigma)-A(\eta_{2};\sigma),

where
\eta_{j}=\eta(\theta_{j};\sigma)
. Equating the two and differentiating in
z
, we
have that

\frac{dh^{-1}(z)}{dz}\frac{\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}=\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}

for all
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. Since the right-hand side is free of
z
, we conclude
that
\frac{dh^{-1}(z)}{dz}
must be a constant. Thus
h^{-1}
—and hence
h
—is
affine.
∎

Appendix OA3
Additional empirical exercises

OA3.1.
Positivity of
s_{0}(\cdot)
in the Opportunity Atlas data

Figure OA3.1.
Estimated conditional variance
s_{0}^{2}(\sigma)
, binned into deciles, with 95% uniform confidence intervals shown.

In the Opportunity Atlas data, we often observe that the estimated conditional variance is
negative:
\hat{s}_{0}^{2}<0
. To test if this is due to sampling variation or underdispersion
of the Opportunity Atlas estimates relative to the estimated standard error, we consider
the following upward-biased estimator of
s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})
. Without loss, let us sort the

Y_{i},\sigma_{i}
by
\sigma_{i}
, where
\sigma_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\sigma_{n}
. Let
S_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\left[{(Y_{i+1}-Y_{i})^{2}-(\sigma_{i}^{2}+\sigma_{i+1}^{2})}\right]
. Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[S_{i}\mid\sigma_{1:n}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[(\theta_{i+1}-\theta_{i})^{2}\mid\sigma_{1:n}]

\displaystyle=\frac{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i+1})+s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})}{2}+\frac{1}{2}(m_{0}(\sigma_{i+1})-m_{0}(\sigma_{i}))^{2}\geq\frac{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i+1})+s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})}{2}.

Hence
S_{i}
is an overestimate of the successive averages of
s_{0}(\sigma)
.
Figure

OA3.1
plot the estimated conditional expectation of

S_{i}
given
\sigma_{i}
, using a sample of
(S_{1},S_{3},S_{5},\ldots)
so that the
S_{i}
’s
used are mutually independent. We see in
Figure

OA3.1
that for many
measures of economic mobility, we can reject
\mathbb{E}[S_{i}\mid\sigma_{i}]\geq 0
, indicating
some underdispersion in the data.

We now describe the data-generating process for the calibrated simulation exercise. For a
given outcome variable,

(CS-1)

Let
\tilde{Y}_{i}
denote the raw estimates.
Residualize
\tilde{Y}_{i}
against some covariates
X_{i}
to obtain
\beta
and residuals
Y_{i}
.

(CS-2)

Estimate the conditional moments
m_{0},s_{0}
on
(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})
via local linear
regression, described in
Appendix

SM3
.

(CS-3)

Partition
\sigma
into vingtiles. Within each
vingtile
j
, estimate an
npmle

G_{j}
over the data in that vingtile

\left({\frac{Y_{i}-m_{0}(\sigma_{i})}{s_{0}(\sigma_{i})},\frac{\sigma_{i}}{s_{0}(\sigma_{i})}}\right).

(CS-4)

Normalize
G_{j}
to
have zero mean and unit variance by shifting its support and dividing the support by
its standard deviation.

(CS-5)

Sample
\tau_{i}^{*}\mid\sigma_{i}\sim G_{j}

if observation
i

falls within vingtile
j
.

(CS-6)

Let
\vartheta_{i}^{*}=s_{0}(\sigma_{i})\tau_{i}^{*}+m_{0}(\sigma_{i})+\beta^{\prime}X_{i}
and
let
\tilde{Y}_{i}^{*}\mid\vartheta_{i}^{*},\sigma_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\vartheta_{i}^{*},\sigma_{i}^{2})
.

The estimated

\beta,m_{0},s_{0}
will serve as
the basis for the true data-generating process in the simulation, and as a result we do
not denote it with hats.

Figure

OA3.2
shows an overlay
of real and simulated data for one of the variables we consider. Visually, at least, the
simulated data resemble the real estimates.

Figure OA3.2.
A draw of real vs. simulated data for estimates of
top-
20
probability
for Black
individuals

Finally, we describe details for the policy exercise. Fix a given outcome variable:

(CB-1)

Let
\tilde{Y}_{i},\sigma_{i}
denote the observed data.

(CB-2)

Define
\tilde{Y}_{i,1}=Y_{i}+\frac{1}{3}\sigma_{i}W_{i}
and
\tilde{Y}_{i,2}=Y_{i}-3\sigma_{i}W_{i}
as the coupled bootstrap draws, for
\omega=1/9
.
Correspondingly, let
\sigma_{i,1}=\sqrt{10/9}\sigma_{i}
and
\sigma_{i,2}=\sqrt{10}\sigma_{i}
.

For the policy exercise, we separate the units into Commuting Zones (CZs). For each CZ
separately, we treat the data as
(\tilde{Y}_{i,1},\sigma_{i,1},X_{i})
within that CZ. We
compute decision rules by only using data within the CZ (including the residualization by
covariates). For the selection exercise, we select by top third within each CZ.

OA3.3.
Different simulation setup

Figure OA3.3.
Analogue of
Figure

4
for the data-generating process in
Section

OA3.3
. Here the results average over 100 replications.

We have also conducted a Monte Carlo exercise where we replace

Item

(CS-3)
–
Item

(CS-5)

with the following step:

•

For each
\sigma_{i}
, let

\alpha_{i}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{m_{0}(\sigma_{i})-\min_{i}m_{0}(\sigma_{i})}{\max_{i}m_{0}(\sigma_{i})-\min_{i}(\sigma_{i})}\in[1/2,1]

We sample
\tau_{i}^{*}\mid\sigma_{i}
as a scaled and translated
\mathrm{Weibull}

distribution with shape
\alpha_{i}
. The scaling and translation ensures that
\tau_{i}\mid\sigma_{i}
has mean zero and variance one. Because we choose the Weibull
distribution, the shape parameter
\alpha_{i}
corresponds exactly to
\alpha
in

2
. Our choices of
\alpha_{i}
implies that
\tau_{i}\mid\sigma_{i}
has thicker tails than exponential and does not have a moment-generating
function.

The Weibull distribution has thicker tails and is skewed, and as
a result,
npmle
-based methods tend to greatly outperform methods based on
assuming Gaussian priors.
Figure

OA3.3
shows the
analogue of
Figure

4
for this data-generating process. Indeed,
we see that
independent-npmle
improves over
independent-gauss
considerably, and
similarly for
close
-
npmle
and
close
-
gauss
.

OA3.4.
Treating
\sigma_{i}
symmetrically with covariates

Here, we consider
close
with covariates (
3.1.2
). In
principle, we could model
m_{0}(\sigma_{i},X_{i}),s_{0}(\sigma_{i},X_{i})
fully
nonparametrically. However, such a model may be difficult to estimate given there are 9
additional covariates. Alternatively, we consider an additive model for the
covariates:
39
39

39

We thank the editor, Michal Kolesár, for this suggestion.

m_{0}(\sigma_{i},X_{i})=g_{0}(\sigma_{i})+\sum_{k}g_{k}(X_{ik})\hskip 10.00002pts_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i},X_{i})=h_{0}(\sigma_{i})+\sum_{k}h_{k}(X_{ik}).

For each covariate and for
\sigma
, the functions
g_{k}(\cdot),h_{k}(\cdot)
are
approximated with cubic splines with knots at the 25
th
, 50
th
, and 75
th

percentiles of the covariate.

We estimate
m_{0}(\cdot)
by least squares projection of
Y_{i}
onto the basis functions in

(\sigma_{i},X_{i})
. We estimate
s_{0}^{2}(\cdot)
by least squares projection of
(Y_{i}-\hat{m}(\sigma_{i},X_{i}))^{2}-\sigma_{i}^{2}
onto the basis functions in
(\sigma_{i},X_{i})
. We
truncate fitted values for
s_{0}^{2}
at zero. In practice, a substantial portion of them are
negative (cf.
Section

OA3.1
). Additionally, we consider a similar
procedure, but one in which
g_{0}(\cdot),h_{0}(\cdot)
are constant. We think of this
procedure as
independent-gauss
with flexible controls for the covariates.

Notes.

“Flexible” denotes procedures that use the additive model (
OA3.4
) for

X_{i}
. “
x
residualized” refers to procedures that, like those in the main text,
residualize against the covariates in a pre-processing step. The percentage in
orange shows the proportion of units with strictly positive estimated conditional
variance
\hat{s}^{2}(\sigma_{i},X_{i})
for the flexible procedures.
∎

Figure OA3.4.
Analogue of
Figure

5
for the setup in

Section

OA3.4
.

We repeat the coupled bootstrap-based ranking exercise in

Figure

5
. However, here, due to considerably more
flexible procedures, we no longer consider a within-Commuting Zone version of the
exercise. Rather, we estimate everything on all the tracts, and select the top third over
all tracts.

Figure

OA3.4
plots the results of this exercise, analogous to

Figure

5
. We find that, compared to the
residualize-then-shrink approach in the main text, modeling
X_{i}
’s additionally well can
have large benefits. We caution that, since much of the conditional signal variance

s_{0}^{2}
appears to be quite small once we use (
OA3.4
) for the
covariates, empirical Bayes posterior means are not very different from using
\hat{m}_{0}(\sigma_{i},X_{i})
directly. Importantly, for this application, including
\sigma_{i}
in
modeling
m_{0},s_{0}
remains important. The approach that simply models
X_{i}
’s flexibly
for
m_{0},s_{0}
but omits
\sigma_{i}
does not outperform the
close
approaches in the
main text.

“Empirical Bayes When Estimation Precision Predicts Parameters”

Jiafeng Chen
