Lemma SM1.12
.

Suppose
Z
has simultaneous moment control
\mathbb{E}[|Z|^{p}]^{1/p}\leq Ap^{1/\alpha}
. Then

\operatorname{P}(|Z|>M)\leq\exp\left({-C_{A,\alpha}M^{\alpha}}\right).

As a corollary, suppose
Z\sim f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(\cdot)
and
G_{0}
obeys
2
,
then

\operatorname{P}(|Z|>M)\leq\exp\left({-C_{A_{0},\alpha,\nu_{u}}M^{\alpha}}\right).

Proof.

Observe that

(Markov’s
inequality)

\displaystyle\operatorname{P}(|Z|>M)=\operatorname{P}(|Z|^{p}>M^{p})\leq\left\{{\frac{Ap^{1/\alpha}}{M}}\right\}^{p}.

Choose
p=(M/(eA))^{\alpha}
such that

\left\{{\frac{Ap^{1/\alpha}}{M}}\right\}^{p}=\exp\left({-p}\right)=\exp\left({-\left({\frac{1}{eA}}\right)^{\alpha}M^{\alpha}}\right).\qed

Lemma SM1.13
.

Let
E
be some event and assume that

\operatorname{P}(E,A>a)\leq p_{1}\hskip 9.24994pt\operatorname{P}(E,B>b)\leq p_{2}

Then
\operatorname{P}(E,A+B>a+b)\leq p_{1}+p_{2}

Proof.

Note that
A+B>a+b
implies that one of
A>a
and
B>b
occurs. Hence

\operatorname{P}(E,A+B>a+b)\leq\operatorname{P}(\left\{{E,A>a}\right\}\cup\left\{{E,B>b}\right\})\leq p_{1}+p_{2}

by union bound.
∎

Lemma SM1.14
.

Let
\tau\sim G_{0}
where
G_{0}
satisfies
2
. Let
Z\mid\tau\sim\mathcal{N}(\tau,\nu^{2})
. Then the posterior moment is
bounded by a power of
|z|
:

\mathbb{E}[|\tau|^{p}\mid Z=z]\lesssim_{p,\alpha,A_{0}}(|z|\vee 1)^{p}.

Proof.

Let
M=|z|\vee 2
. We write

\mathbb{E}[|\tau|^{p}\mid Z=z]=\frac{1}{f_{G_{0},\nu}(z)}\int|\tau|^{p}\varphi\left({\frac{z-\tau}{\nu}}\right)\frac{1}{\nu}G_{0}(d\tau).

Note that we can decompose based on
|\tau|>3M
:

\displaystyle\int|\tau|^{p}\varphi\left({\frac{z-\tau}{\nu}}\right)\frac{1}{\nu}G_{0}(d\tau)

\displaystyle\leq(3M)^{p}f_{G_{0},\nu}(z)+\int\mathbbm{1}(|\tau|>3M)|\tau|^{p}\varphi\left({\frac{z-\tau}{\nu}}\right)\frac{1}{\nu}G_{0}(d\tau)

(
|z-\tau|\geq 2M
when
|\tau|>3M
)

\displaystyle\leq(3M)^{p}f_{G_{0},\nu}(z)+\int_{|\tau|>3M}|\tau|^{p}G_{0}(d\tau)\cdot\frac{1}{\nu}\varphi\left({|2M|/\nu}\right)

Also note that, since
|z|\leq M
,

(
|z-\tau|\leq 2M
if
\tau\in[-M,M]
)

\displaystyle f_{G_{0},\nu}(z)=\int\varphi\left({\frac{z-\tau}{\nu}}\right)\frac{1}{\nu}G_{0}(d\tau)\geq\int_{-M}^{M}\varphi\left({\frac{z-\tau}{\nu}}\right)\frac{1}{\nu}G_{0}(d\tau)\geq\frac{1}{\nu}\varphi\left({|2M|/\nu}\right)G_{0}([-M,M])

Hence,

\mathbb{E}[|\tau|^{p}\mid Z=z]\leq(3M)^{p}+\frac{\int|\tau|^{p}G_{0}(d\tau)}{G_{0}([-M,M])}.

Since
G_{0}
is mean zero and variance 1, by Chebyshev’s inequality,
G_{0}([-M,M])\geq G_{0}([-2,2])\geq 3/4
. Hence
\mathbb{E}[|\tau|^{p}\mid Z=z]\lesssim_{p,\alpha,A_{0}}M^{p}\lesssim_{p,\alpha,A_{0}}(|z|\vee 1)^{p}
,
since we have bounded
p
th
moments by
2
.
∎

Appendix SM2
A large-deviation inequality for the average Hellinger distance

Theorem SM2.1
.

For some
n\geq 7
, let
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n}\mid(\nu_{1}^{2},\ldots,\nu_{n}^{2})\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}G_{0}

where
G_{0}

satisfies
2
.
Let
\nu_{u}=\max_{i}\nu_{i}
and
\nu_{\ell}=\min_{i}\nu_{i}
.
Assume
Z_{i}\mid\tau_{i},\nu_{i}^{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(\tau_{i},\nu_{i}^{2})
.
Fix positive sequences
\gamma_{n},\lambda_{n}\to 0
with
\gamma_{n},\lambda_{n}\leq 1
and
constant
\epsilon>0
. Fix some positive constant
C^{*}
.
Consider the set of distributions that approximately maximize the likelihood

A(\gamma_{n},\lambda_{n})=\left\{{H\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}):\mathrm{Sub}_{n}(H)\leq C^{*}\left({\gamma_{n}^{2}+\overline{h}(f_{H,\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})\lambda_{n}}\right)}\right\}.

Also consider the set of distributions that are far from
G_{0}
in
\overline{h}
:

B(t,\lambda_{n},\epsilon)=\left\{{H\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}):\overline{h}(f_{H,\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})\geq tB\lambda_{n}^{1-\epsilon}}\right\}

with some constant
B
to be chosen.
Assume that for some
C_{\lambda}
,

\lambda_{n}^{2}\geq\left({\frac{C_{\lambda}}{n}(\log n)^{1+\frac{\alpha+2}{2\alpha}}}\right)\vee\gamma_{n}^{2}.

Then the probability that
A\cap B
is nonempty is bounded for
t>1
: There exists
a choice of
B
that depends only on
\nu_{\ell},\nu_{u},C^{*},C_{\lambda}
such that

\operatorname{P}\left[{A(\gamma_{n},\lambda_{n})\cap B(t,\lambda_{n},\epsilon)\neq\varnothing}\right]\leq(\log_{2}(1/\epsilon)+1)n^{-t^{2}}.

See
OA1.1

Proof.

Let
\gamma=\frac{2+\alpha}{2\alpha}+\beta
. We first note that, for

\varepsilon_{n}

in
(
SM1.1
), the choices

\lambda_{n}=n^{-p/(2p+1)}(\log n)^{\frac{2+\alpha}{2\alpha}+\beta}\wedge 1=\gamma_{n}

do satisfy (
SM2.1
). Note that the choices of
\lambda_{n},\gamma_{n}
are such that
\varepsilon_{n}\leq C_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{n}\overline{h}+\gamma_{n}^{2})
.

The event
\left\{{A_{n},\overline{h}(f_{\hat{G}_{n},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})>t\delta_{n}}\right\}
is a subset of the union
of

E_{1}=\left\{{A_{n},\mathrm{Sub}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n})>C_{\mathcal{H}}^{*}\varepsilon_{n}}\right\}\text{ and }E_{2}=\left\{{A_{n},\mathrm{Sub}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n})\leq C_{\mathcal{H}}^{*}\varepsilon_{n},\overline{h}(f_{\hat{G}_{n},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})>tn^{-p/(2p+1)}(\log n)^{\gamma}}\right\}.

Thus
\operatorname{P}\left[{A_{n},\overline{h}(f_{\hat{G}_{n},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})>t\delta_{n}}\right]\leq\operatorname{P}(E_{1})+\operatorname{P}(E_{2})
.

SM1.1
implies that
\operatorname{P}(E_{1})\leq 9/n
.

Now, note that

\displaystyle\operatorname{P}(E_{2})\leq\operatorname{P}\left[{A_{n},\mathrm{Sub}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n})\leq C_{\mathcal{H}}^{*}C_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{n}\overline{h}+\gamma_{n}^{2}),\overline{h}(f_{\hat{G}_{n},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})\geq t\lambda_{n}}\right].

Observe that, for
\epsilon=1/\log(n)

\displaystyle t\lambda_{n}^{1-\epsilon}

\displaystyle=t\left[{n^{-\frac{p}{2p+1}(1-\epsilon)}(\log n)^{\gamma(1-\epsilon)}\wedge 1}\right]

\displaystyle=t\left({n^{-\frac{p}{2p+1}}(\log n)^{\gamma}\left[{n^{\frac{\epsilon p}{2p+1}}(\log n)^{-\gamma\epsilon}}\right]\wedge 1}\right)

\displaystyle=t\left({n^{-\frac{p}{2p+1}}(\log n)^{\gamma}\left[{e^{\frac{p}{2p+1}}(\log n)^{-\gamma\epsilon}}\right]\wedge 1}\right)

(
e^{\frac{p}{2p+1}}(\log n)^{-\gamma\epsilon}
is bounded by a constant)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,\gamma}t\lambda_{n}

Thus, by
Theorem

SM2.1
, for all sufficiently large
t
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{P}(E_{2})

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{P}\left[{\mathrm{Sub}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n})\leq C_{\mathcal{H}}^{*}C_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{n}\overline{h}(f_{\hat{G}_{n},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})+\gamma_{n}^{2}),\overline{h}(f_{\hat{G}_{n},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})\geq\frac{t}{C_{p,\gamma}}\lambda_{n}^{1-\epsilon}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{P}\left[{A(\gamma_{n},\lambda_{n})\cap B\left({\frac{t}{BC_{p,\lambda}},\lambda_{n},\epsilon}\right)\neq\varnothing}\right]\leq\left({\log_{2}(\log n)+1}\right)n^{-t^{2}/C_{\mathcal{H}}}

We can pick
t=B_{\mathcal{H}}
sufficiently large such that
n^{-t^{2}/C_{\mathcal{H}}}\leq 1/n
and

\operatorname{P}\left[{A_{n},\overline{h}(f_{\hat{G}_{n},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})>t\delta_{n}}\right]\leq\operatorname{P}(E_{1})+\operatorname{P}(E_{2})\leq\left({\frac{\log\log n}{\log 2}+10}\right)\frac{1}{n}.\qed

SM2.1.
Proof of
Theorem

SM2.1

SM2.1.1.
Decompose
B(t,\lambda_{n},\epsilon)

We decompose
B(t,\lambda_{n},\epsilon)\subset\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}B_{k}(t,\lambda_{n})

where, for some constant
B>1
to be chosen,

B_{k}=\left\{{H:\overline{h}\left({f_{H,\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}}}\right)\in\left(tB\lambda_{n}^{1-2^{-k}},tB\lambda_{n}^{1-2^{-k+1}}\right]}\right\}.

The relation
B(t,\lambda_{n},\epsilon)\subset\bigcup_{k}B_{k}
holds if we take
K=\lceil|\log_{2}(1/\epsilon)|\rceil
.

In the remainder, we will bound

\operatorname{P}(A(\gamma_{n},\lambda_{n})\cap B_{k}(t,\lambda_{n})\neq\varnothing)\leq n^{-t^{2}}

which becomes the bound (
SM2.1
) by a union bound. This argument
follows the argument for Theorem 7 in
Soloff et al. 2024h

and Theorem 4 in
Jiang 2020h
. For
k\in[K]
, define
\mu_{n,k}=B\lambda_{n}^{1-2^{-k+1}}
such that
B_{k}=\left\{{H:\overline{h}\left({f_{H,\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}}}\right)\in\left(t\mu_{n,k+1},t\mu_{n,k}\right]}\right\}.
To that end, fix some
k
.

SM2.1.2.
Construct a net for the set of densities
f_{G}

Fix a positive constant
M
and define the pseudonorm

\lVert G\rVert_{\infty,M}=\max_{i\in[n]}\sup_{y\in[-M,M]}f_{G,\nu_{i}}(y).

Note that
\lVert G_{1}-G_{2}\rVert_{\infty,M}\asymp_{\mathcal{H}}d_{\infty,M}^{(0)}(G_{1},G_{2})

defined in

SM1.1
. Fix
\omega=\frac{1}{n^{2}}>0
and consider an

\omega
-net for
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
under
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty,M}
. Let

N=N(\omega,\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty,M})
and the
\omega
-net consists of the distributions
H_{1},\ldots,H_{N}
.
For each
j\in[N]
, let
H_{k,j}
be a distribution, if it exists, with

\lVert H_{k,j}-H_{j}\rVert_{\infty,M}\leq\omega\hskip 9.24994pt\overline{h}(f_{H_{k,j},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})\geq t\mu_{n,k+1}

and let
J_{k}
collect the indices
j
for which
H_{j,k}
exists.

SM2.1.3.
Project to the net and upper bound the likelihood

Fix a distribution
H\in B_{k}(t,\lambda_{n})
. There exists some member of the covering,

H_{j}
, such that
\lVert H-H_{j}\rVert_{\infty,M}\leq\omega
. Moreover,
H
serves as a witness that
H_{k,j}

exists, with
\lVert H-H_{k,j}\rVert_{\infty,M}\leq 2\omega.

We can construct an upper bound for
f_{H,\nu_{i}}(z)
via

f_{H,\nu_{i}}(z)\leq\begin{cases}f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}(z)+2\omega&|z|\leq M\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\nu_{i}}&|z|>M.\end{cases}

Define
v(z)=\omega\mathbbm{1}(|z|\leq M)+\frac{\omega M^{2}}{z^{2}}\mathbbm{1}(|z|>M).
Observe
that

\displaystyle f_{H,\nu_{i}}(z)\leq\begin{cases}f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}(z)+2v(z)&|z|\leq M\\
\frac{f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}(z)+2v(z)}{\sqrt{2\pi}\nu_{i}v(z)}&|z|>M.\end{cases}

Hence, the likelihood ratio between
H
and
G_{0}
is upper bounded:

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{f_{H,\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})+2v(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}\prod_{i:|Z_{i}|>M}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\nu_{i}v(Z_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\left({\max_{j\in J_{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})+2v(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}}\right)\prod_{i:|Z_{i}|>M}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\nu_{i}v(Z_{i})}

If
H\in A(t,\gamma_{n},\lambda_{n})
, then the likelihood ratio is also lower bounded:

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{f_{H,\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left({-nC^{*}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\overline{h}\left({f_{H,\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}}}\right)\lambda_{n})}\right)

(
t>1
)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left({-ntC^{*}(t\gamma_{n}^{2}+\overline{h}\left({f_{H,\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}}}\right)\lambda_{n})}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left({-nC^{*}(t^{2}\gamma_{n}^{2}+t\overline{h}\lambda_{n})}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left({-nC^{*}(t^{2}\gamma_{n}^{2}+t^{2}\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})}\right).

Hence,

\displaystyle\operatorname{P}\left[{A(t,\gamma_{n},\lambda_{n})\cap B_{k}(t,\lambda_{n})\neq\varnothing}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{P}\Bigg\{\left({\max_{j\in J_{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})+2v(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}}\right)\prod_{i:|Z_{i}|>M}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\nu_{i}v(Z_{i})}\geq\exp\left({-nt^{2}C^{*}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})}\right)\Bigg\}

(SM2.4)

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{P}\left[{\max_{j\in J_{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}+2v(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}\geq e^{-nt^{2}aC^{*}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})}}\right]

(SM2.5)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt+\operatorname{P}\left[{\prod_{i:|Z_{i}|>M}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\nu_{i}v(Z_{i})}\geq e^{nt^{2}(a-1)C^{*}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})}}\right]

The second
inequality follows from choosing some
a>1
and applying union bound.

SM2.1.4.
Bounding (
SM2.4
)

We consider bounding the first term
(
SM2.4
) now:

(Union bound)

(
SM2.4
)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\operatorname{P}\left[{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}+2v(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}\geq e^{-nat^{2}C^{*}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})}}\right]

(Take square root
of both sides, then apply Markov’s inequality)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\frac{f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})+2v(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}}}\right]e^{nat^{2}C^{*}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})/2}

(SM2.6)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in J_{k}}e^{nat^{2}C^{*}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})/2}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[{\sqrt{\frac{f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})+2v(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}}}\right]

where the last step (
SM2.6
) is by independence over
i
.
Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sqrt{\frac{f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})+2v(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Y_{i})}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sqrt{f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}(x)+2v(x)}\sqrt{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(x)}\,dx

(
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
)

\displaystyle\leq 1-h^{2}(f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}},f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}})+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\underbrace{\sqrt{2v(x)f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(x)}}_{\sqrt{2v(x)/f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}}\cdot f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}}\,dx

(Jensen’s inequality)

\displaystyle\leq 1-h^{2}(f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}},f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}})+\left({2\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}v(x)\,dx}\right)^{1/2}

(Direct integration)

\displaystyle=1-h^{2}(f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}},f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}})+\sqrt{8M\omega}

Also note that, for
t_{i}>0
, we have

\prod_{i=1}^{n}t_{i}=\exp\left({\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log t_{i}}\right)\leq\exp\left({\sum_{i=1}^{n}(t_{i}-1)}\right).

and thus

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[{\sqrt{\frac{f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}+2v(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}}}\right]\leq\exp\left[{-n\overline{h}^{2}(f_{H_{k,j},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})+n\sqrt{8M\omega}}\right].

Thus, we can further bound (
SM2.6
):

(
SM2.4
)

\displaystyle\leq\eqref{eq:term1_inter}=\sum_{j\in J_{k}}e^{n\alpha t^{2}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})/2}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[{\sqrt{\frac{f_{H_{k,j},\nu_{i}}+2v(Z_{i})}{f_{G_{0},\nu_{i}}(Z_{i})}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\exp\left\{{\frac{nat^{2}C^{*}}{2}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})-n\overline{h}^{2}(f_{H_{k,j},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})+n\sqrt{8M\omega}}\right\}

(
\overline{h}^{2}(f_{H_{k,j},\bm{\cdot}},f_{G_{0},\bm{\cdot}})\geq t\mu_{n,k+1}
by (
SM2.1.2
))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in J_{k}}\exp\left\{{\frac{nat^{2}C^{*}}{2}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})-nt^{2}\mu_{n,k+1}^{2}+n\sqrt{8M\omega}}\right\}

(
|J_{k}|\leq N
)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left\{{\frac{nat^{2}C^{*}}{2}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})-nt^{2}\mu_{n,k+1}^{2}+n\sqrt{8M\omega}+\log N}\right\}

(
SM1.1
,
q=0
)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left\{{\frac{nat^{2}C^{*}}{2}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})-nt^{2}\mu_{n,k+1}^{2}+n\sqrt{8M\omega}+C\lvert\log\omega\rvert^{2}\max\left({\frac{M}{\sqrt{|\log\omega|}},1}\right)}\right\}

(Recall that
\omega=\frac{1}{n^{2}}
)

\displaystyle=\exp\left\{{\frac{nat^{2}C^{*}}{2}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})-nt^{2}\mu_{n,k+1}^{2}+\sqrt{8M}+C(\log n)^{2}\max\left({\frac{M}{\sqrt{\log n}},1}\right)}\right\}.

SM2.1.5.
Bounding (
SM2.5
)

We now consider bounding the second term (
SM2.5
). By Markov’s inequality again
(taking
x\mapsto x^{1/(2\log n)}
on both sides
),
we can choose to bound

\eqref{eq:term2}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left({\frac{1}{(2\pi\nu_{i}^{2})^{1/4}}\frac{Z_{i}}{M\sqrt{\omega}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\log n}\mathbbm{1}(|Z_{i}|>M)}}\right]\exp\left({-\frac{n(a-1)t^{2}C^{*}(\gamma_{n}^{2}+\mu_{n,k}\lambda_{n})}{2\log n}}\right)

instead. Define

a_{i}=\frac{1}{(2\pi\nu_{i}^{2})^{1/4}M\sqrt{\omega}}\leq\frac{C_{\nu_{\ell}}n}{M}\hskip 9.24994pt\lambda=\frac{1}{\log n}

Apply
Lemma

SM2.1
to obtain the following. Note that to do so, we require
M\geq\nu_{u}\sqrt{8\log n}
and
p\geq\frac{1}{\log n}.
Then,

\displaystyle\log\mathbb{E}\left[{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left({\frac{1}{(2\pi\nu_{i}^{2})^{1/4}}\frac{Z_{i}}{M\sqrt{\omega}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\log n}\mathbbm{1}(|Z_{i}|>M)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\log\mathbb{E}\left[{\prod_{i}(a_{i}Z_{i})^{\lambda\mathbbm{1}(|Z_{i}|\geq M)}}\right]

(
Lemma

SM2.1
)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\nu_{u}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}M)^{\lambda}\left({\frac{1}{Mn}+\frac{2^{p}\mu_{p}^{p}(G_{0})}{M^{p}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}(C_{\nu_{\ell}}n)^{\frac{1}{\log n}}\left({\frac{1}{Mn}+\frac{2^{p}\mu_{p}^{p}(G_{0})}{M^{p}}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\nu_{u},\nu_{\ell}}\frac{1}{M}+n\frac{2^{p}\mu_{p}^{p}(G_{0})}{M^{p}}

As a result,

(SM2.7)

\displaystyle\log[\eqref{eq:term2}]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\nu_{u},\nu_{\ell}}\left({\frac{1}{M}+\frac{2^{p}n\mu_{p}^{p}(G_{0})}{M^{p}}}\right)-\frac{n(a-1)}{2\log n}t^{2}C^{*}\left({\gamma_{n}^{2}+B\lambda_{n}^{2(1-2^{-k})}}\right).

To conclude, note that by
2
,
\mu_{p}^{p}(G_{0})\leq A_{0}^{p}p^{p/\alpha}
. Let
M=2eA_{0}(c_{m}\log n)^{1/\alpha}
and
p=(M/(2eA_{0}))^{1/\alpha}
so that

2^{p}\mu_{p}^{p}(G_{0})/M^{p}\leq\exp\left({-c_{m}\log n}\right)

We choose
c_{m}\geq 2
sufficiently large such that
M=2eA_{0}(c_{m}\log n)^{1/\alpha}>\nu_{u}\sqrt{8\log n}\vee 1
and
p\geq 1
for all
n>2
to ensure that our application of

Lemma

SM2.1
is correct. We also choose
a=1.5
.

Plugging in these choices, we can verify that, via (
SM2.1
),

\displaystyle\log[\eqref{eq:term2}]

\displaystyle\leq t^{2}\left[{2C_{\nu_{u},\nu_{\ell}}-\frac{C^{*}BC_{\lambda}}{4}\left({\log n}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\log[\eqref{eq:term1}]

\displaystyle\leq-t^{2}(\log n)^{1+\frac{2+\alpha}{2\alpha}}\left[{C_{\lambda}\left({-\frac{3}{4}C^{*}-\frac{3}{4}C^{*}B+B^{2}}\right)-C}\right]

There exists a sufficiently large choice of
B
such that
\log[\eqref{eq:term2}]\leq-t^{2}\log n-\log 2
and
\log[\eqref{eq:term1}]\leq-t^{2}\log n-\log 2
. Thus, we
obtain that

\eqref{eq:term1}+\eqref{eq:term2}\leq n^{-t^{2}}.

This concludes the proof.

SM2.2.
Auxiliary lemmas

Lemma SM2.1

(Lemma 5,
Jiang 2020h
)
.

Suppose
Z_{i}\mid\tau_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\tau_{i},\nu_{i}^{2})
where
\tau_{i}\mid\nu_{i}^{2}\sim G_{0}

independently across
i
. Let
0<\nu_{u},\nu_{\ell}<\infty
be the upper and lower bounds
for
\nu_{i}
. Then,
for all constants
M>0,\lambda>0,a_{i}>0,p\in\mathbb{N}

such that
M\geq\nu_{u}\sqrt{8\log n}
,

\lambda\in(0,p\wedge 1)
, and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}>0
:

\mathbb{E}\left\{{\prod_{i}|a_{i}Z_{i}|^{\lambda\mathbbm{1}(|Z_{i}|\geq M)}}\right\}\leq\exp\left\{{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}M)^{\lambda}\left({\frac{4\nu_{u}}{Mn\sqrt{2\pi}}+\left({\frac{2\mu_{p}(G_{0})}{M}}\right)^{p}}\right)}\right\},

where
\mu_{p}^{p}(G_{0})=\int|\tau|^{p}G_{0}(d\tau)
.

Part IV
Additional theoretical results

Appendix SM3
Estimating
\eta_{0}
by local linear regression

This section details how we estimate
\eta_{0}=(m_{0}(\cdot),s_{0}(\cdot))
by local linear
regression in
Section

4
. It also outlines a detailed procedure and verifies
that this procedure satisfies the conditions we require for the conditional moment
estimation, when the true
\eta_{0}
belong to a Hölder class of order
p=2
:

m_{0}(\sigma),s_{0}(\sigma)\in C_{A_{1}}^{2}([\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{u}])
.

In our empirical application, we estimate
m_{0},s_{0}
by nonparametrically regressing
Y_{i}

on
x_{i}\equiv\log_{10}(\sigma_{i})
.
40
40

40

Correspondingly, let
\sigma(x)=10^{x}.

Our procedure takes the following steps, which simply use kernel-based nonparametric
regression procedures implemented by
Calonico et al. 2019e

to estimate
m_{0}
and
s_{0}
and truncate the estimated
s_{0}
below at some data-driven
point. Nonparametric regression is frequently applied to visualize data and to estimate
causal effects in regression discontinuity settings.

(E-1)

Use the default procedure
Calonico et al. 2019e
to
estimate
local linear regression of
Y_{i}
on
x_{i}
(Epanechnikov kernel, IMSE direct plug-in
bandwidth). The resulting estimated conditional mean is

\hat{m}(\cdot)
.

(E-2)

Let
\hat{R}_{i}^{2}=(Y_{i}-\hat{m}(x_{i}))^{2}
. Use the above local linear regression
procedure again to estimate the conditional mean of
\hat{R}_{i}^{2}
on
x_{i}
, and let

\hat{v}(x)
be the estimated conditional mean. Let

\tilde{s}^{2}(\sigma_{i})=\hat{v}(x_{i})-\sigma_{i}^{2}.

(E-3)

Since
\hat{v}(x_{i})
is a linear smoother, it can be written as

\hat{v}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}(x)\hat{R}_{i}^{2}.

for some weights
\ell_{i}(x)
.
Let an estimate of the effective sample size be

p_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\ell_{i}^{2}(x_{j})}.

(E-4)

Let the estimated conditional variance be

\hat{s}^{2}(\sigma_{i})=\tilde{s}^{2}(\sigma_{i})\vee\frac{2}{p_{n}+2}\left({\hat{v}(x_{i})\vee\min_{j=1,\ldots,n}\sigma_{j}^{2}}\right)

where the additional truncation by
\min_{i=1,\ldots,n}\sigma_{i}^{2}
deals with the
unlikely scenario that
\hat{v}(x_{i})
is negative. Note that, in theory, the population
analogue
v(x_{i})=\mathbb{E}[R_{i}^{2}\mid x_{i}]=s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})+\sigma_{i}\geq\sigma_{i}^{2}
. See
SM3.1

for a heuristic rationale of the above truncation rule.

The rest of the section analyzes the theoretical properties of a similar procedure for
analyzing
m_{0}(\cdot),s_{0}(\cdot)
and connects them to the requirements in

4
. The product of the analysis is
Theorem

SM3.2
, which
verifies the same regret bound as in
Theorem

1
, where we estimate
m_{0},s_{0}

with the procedure we outline below.

There are a few minor inconveniences of the
above
procedure that we
strengthen below:

•

We would like to control for the fact that the bandwidths
\hat{h}_{n}
for the local
linear
regression is data-driven. However, to establish uniform behavior in
\hat{h}_{n}
, we
would like to restrict it to satisfy the optimal convergence rate almost surely:
For some
C>0
,

C^{-1}n^{-1/5}\leq\hat{h}_{n}\leq Cn^{-1/5}\text{ almost surely.}

•

We would like to ensure that the estimated functions
\hat{m},\hat{s}
are Hölder
continuous almost surely. Since
m_{0}(x),s_{0}(x)
are Hölder continuous,
41
41

41

Since
\log(\cdot)
is a smooth transformation on strictly positive compact sets,
Hölder smoothness conditions for
(m_{0},s_{0})
translate to the same conditions on
(\mathbb{E}[Y\mid x],\operatorname{Var}(Y\mid x)-\sigma^{2}(x))
, with potentially different constants.
Moreover, scaling and translating
x_{i}
linearly do not affect our technical results.
As a result, we assume, without essential loss of generality,
x_{i}\in[0,1]
. We abuse
and recycle notation to write
m_{0}(x)=\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\mid x_{i}=x],s_{0}(x)=\operatorname{Var}(\theta_{i}\mid x_{i}=x)
. We also note that
m_{0}(x),s_{0}(x)\in C_{A_{3}}^{2}([0,1])
for
some
A_{3}\lesssim_{\mathcal{H}}A_{1}
.
we do not
significantly
incur estimation error if
we project to Hölder continuous functions.

We enforce these properties in the below procedure that we analyze. We anticipate the
projection steps to be unnecessary in practice and hold with high probability in theory.
Precisely, we add the steps

Items

(LLR-2)
,
(LLR-7)
,
(LLR-4)
, and
(LLR-11)

to the procedure in
Item

(E-1)
–
Item

(E-4)
. We also make the dependence on the
selected bandwidths explicit:

(LLR-1)

Fix some kernel
K(\cdot)
. Use the direct plug-in procedure of

Calonico et al. 2019e
to estimate a
bandwidth
\hat{h}_{n,m}
.

(LLR-2)

For some
C_{h}>1
, project
\hat{h}_{n,m}
to
some interval
[C_{h}^{-1}n^{-1/5},C_{h}n^{-1/5}]
so as to
enforce that it converges at the optimal rate:
42
42

42

We use the
\leftarrow
notation to
reassign a variable so that we can reduce notation clutter.

\hat{h}_{n,m}\leftarrow(\hat{h}_{n,m}\vee C_{h}^{-1}n^{-1/5})\wedge C_{h}n^{-1/5}.

(LLR-3)

Using
\hat{h}_{n,m}
, estimate
m_{0}
with the local
linear regression estimator

\hat{m}_{\mathrm{raw}}
under kernel
K(\cdot)
and bandwidth
\hat{h}_{n,m}
.

(LLR-4)

Project the resulting estimator
\hat{m}
to the
Hölder class
C_{A_{3}}^{2}([0,1])
:

\hat{m}\in\argmin_{m\in C_{A_{3}}^{2}([0,1])}\lVert m-\hat{m}_{\mathrm{raw}}\rVert_{\infty}.

We obtain
\hat{m}
through this procedure.

(LLR-5)

Form estimated squared residuals
\hat{R}_{i}^{2}=(Y_{i}-\hat{m}(x_{i}))^{2}
.

(LLR-6)

Repeat
Item

(LLR-1)
on data
(\hat{R}_{i}^{2},x_{i})
to obtain a bandwidth
\hat{h}_{n,s}
.

(LLR-7)

Repeat
Item

(LLR-2)
to project

\hat{h}_{n,s}
.

(LLR-8)

Using
\hat{h}_{n,s}
, estimate
v(x)=\mathbb{E}[R_{i}^{2}\mid X=x]
with the local linear
regression estimator
\hat{v}
under kernel
K(\cdot)
.

(LLR-9)

Since
\hat{v}
is a local linear regression estimator, it can be written as a
linear smoother
\hat{v}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}(x;\hat{h}_{n,s})\hat{R}_{i}^{2}.

Let an estimate of the effective sample size be

p_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\ell_{i}^{2}(x_{j},\hat{h}_{n,s})}.

(LLR-10)

Truncate the estimated conditional standard deviation:

\hat{s}_{\mathrm{raw}}(x)=\sqrt{\hat{v}(x)-\sigma^{2}(x)}\vee\sqrt{\frac{2}{p_{n}+2}\hat{v}(x)}.

(LLR-11)

Finally, project the resulting estimate to the
Hölder class as in

Item

(LLR-4)
:

\hat{s}(x)\in\argmin_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in C_{A_{3}}^{2}([0,1])\\
s^{2}(\cdot)\geq\frac{2}{p_{n}+2}\min_{i}\sigma_{i}^{2}\end{subarray}}\lVert s-\hat{s}_{\mathrm{raw}}\rVert_{\infty}.

To ensure we always have a positive estimate of
s_{0}
, we truncate at a particular point
(
(LLR-10)
). This truncation rule is a heuristic (and improper) application
of results from the literature on estimating non-centrality parameters. We digress and
discuss the truncation rule in the next remark.

Remark SM3.1

(The truncation rule in (
(LLR-10)
))
.

The truncation rule in (
(LLR-10)
) is an ad hoc adjustment without affecting asymptotic
performance.
43
43

43

Indeed, since we already assumed that the true conditional variance

s_{0}(x)>s_{\ell}
, we can truncate by any vanishing sequence. Given any
vanishing sequence, eventually it is lower than
s_{\ell}
, and eventually
|\hat{s}-s_{0}|
is small enough for the truncation to not bind. This is, in some sense,
silly, since finite sample performance is likely affected if we truncate by, say,

\frac{1}{\log\log n}
, reflected in a large constant in the corresponding rate
expression. Our following argument assumes that the truncation of order
O(n^{-4/5})
.
Doing so is likely to achieve a smaller constant in the rate expression, despite not
mattering asymptotically.
It is based on a literature on the estimation of non-central

\chi^{2}
parameters
(
Kubokawa et al. 1993e
)
. Specifically, let
U_{i}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(\lambda_{i},1)
and let
V=\sum_{i=1}^{p}U_{i}^{2}
be a noncentral
\chi^{2}
random
variable with
p
degrees of freedom and noncentrality parameter
\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\lambda_{i}^{2}
. The UMVUE for
\lambda
is
V-p,
which is dominated by its positive
part
(V-p)_{+}
.
Kubokawa et al. 1993e
derive a class of estimators of the form

V-\phi(V;p)
that dominate
(V-p)_{+}
in squared error risk. An estimator in this
class is
(V-p)\vee\frac{2}{p+2}V
.
44
44

44

Though, since neither
(V-p)_{+}
nor

(V-p)\vee\frac{2}{p+2}V
is differentiable in
V
, they are not
admissible.

This setting is loosely connected to ours. Suppose
m_{0}
is known, and we were using
a Nadaraya–Watson estimator with uniform kernel. Then, for a given evaluation point

x_{0}
, we would be averaging nearby
R_{i}^{2}
’s. Each
R_{i}
is conditionally Gaussian,

R_{i}\mid(\theta_{i},\sigma_{i})\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta_{i}-m_{0}(\sigma_{i}),\sigma_{i}^{2})
with approximately equal variance
\sigma_{i}^{2}\approx\sigma(x_{0})^{2}
. If there happens to be
p_{0}

R_{i}^{2}
’s that we are averaging, the
Nadaraya–Watson estimator is of the form

\hat{v}(x_{0})=\frac{\sigma(x_{0})^{2}}{p_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\left({\frac{R_{i}}{\sigma(x_{0})}}\right)^{2}

Conditional on
\sigma_{i}^{2},\theta_{i}
, the quantity
\sum_{i=1}^{p}\left({\frac{R_{i}}{\sigma(x_{0})}}\right)^{2}

is (approximately) noncentral
\chi^{2}
with
p
degrees of freedom and noncentrality
parameter

\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{p_{0}}\left({\frac{\theta_{i}-m_{0}(x_{i})}{\sigma(x_{0})}}\right)^{2}

Therefore, correspondingly, applying the truncation rule from

Kubokawa et al. 1993e
, an estimator for the sample variance of
\theta_{i}
,
\frac{1}{p_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{p_{0}}(\theta_{i}-m_{0}(x_{i}))^{2},
is

\left({\hat{v}(x_{0})-\sigma^{2}(x_{0})}\right)\vee\frac{2}{p_{0}+2}\hat{v}(x_{0}).

Here, we apply this truncation rule (improperly) to the case where
\hat{v}(x_{0})
is a
weighted average of the squared residuals, with potentially negative weights due to
higher-order polynomials in the local polynomial regression. To do so, we would need to
plug
in an analogue of
p_{0}
. We note that when independent random variables
V_{i}
have unit
variance, the weighted average
has variance equal to the squared length of the weights

\operatorname{Var}\left({\sum_{i}\ell_{i}(x)V_{i}}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}^{2}(x).

Since a simple average has variance equal to
1/n
, we can take
\left({\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}^{2}(x)}\right)^{-1}
to be an effective sample size. Our rule simply takes the average
effective sample size over evaluation points in (
(LLR-9)
) and use
it as a candidate for
p
.

\blacksquare

The goal in this section is to control the following probability as a function of
t>0

\operatorname{P}\left({\lVert\hat{\eta}-\eta_{0}\rVert_{\infty}>C_{\mathcal{H}}tn^{-2/5}(\log n)^{\beta}}\right)

for some constants
\beta,C_{\mathcal{H}}
to be chosen. Since we treat
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
as fixed (fixed design), we shall do so placing some assumptions on sequences of the
design points
x_{1:n}
as a function of
n
. These assumptions are mild and satisfied
when the design points are equally spaced. They are also satisfied with high probability
when the design points are drawn from a well-behaved density
f(\cdot)
.

Before doing so, we introduce some notation on the local linear regression estimator. Note
that, by translating and scaling if necessary, it is without essential loss of generality
to assume
x_{i}
take values in
[0,1]
. Let
h_{n}
denote some (possibly data-driven) choice of bandwidth.
Let
u(x)=[1,x]^{\prime}
and let
B_{nx}=B_{nx}(h_{n})=\frac{1}{nh_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)u\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)u\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)^{\prime}
. Then, it is easy to see that the
local linear regression weights can be written in terms of
B_{nx}
and
u(\cdot)
:

s_{n}\equiv nh_{n}\hskip 9.24994pt\ell_{i}(x)=\ell_{i}(x,h_{n})\equiv\frac{1}{s_{n}}u(0)^{\prime}B_{nx}^{-1}u\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)K\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right).

We shall maintain the following assumptions on the design points. The following
assumptions introduce constants
(C_{h},n_{0},\lambda_{0},a_{0},K_{0},K(\cdot),c,C,C_{K},V_{K})
which we shall take as primitives like those in
\mathcal{H}
. The symbols
\lesssim,\gtrsim,\asymp
are relative to these constants, and we will not keep track of exact
dependencies through subscripts.

Assumption SM3.1
.

For some constant
C_{h}>1
, the data-driven bandwidth
h_{n}
is almost surely contained in
the set
H_{n}\equiv[C_{h}^{-1}n^{-1/5}\vee\frac{1}{2n},C_{h}n^{-1/5}]
.

SM3.1
is automatically satisfied by the projection steps

Items

(LLR-2)
and
(LLR-7)
.

Assumption SM3.2
.

The sequence of design points
(x_{i}:i=1,\ldots,n)
satisfy:

(1)

There exists a real number
\lambda_{0}>0
and integer
n_{0}>0
such that, for
all
n\geq n_{0}
, any
x\in[0,1]
, and any
\tilde{h}\in[C_{h}^{-1}n^{-1/5}\vee\frac{1}{2n},C_{h}n^{-1/5}]
, the
smallest eigenvalue
\lambda_{\min}(B_{nx}(\tilde{h}))\geq\lambda_{0}
.

(2)

There exists a real number
a_{0}>0
such that for any interval
I\subset[0,1]

and all
n\geq 1
,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}(x_{i}\in I)\leq a_{0}\left({\lambda(I)\vee\frac{1}{n}}\right)

where
\lambda(I)
is the Lebesgue measure of
I
.

(3)

The kernel
K
is supported on
[-1,1]
and uniformly bounded by some
positive constant
K_{0}
.

(4)

There exists
c,C,n_{0}>0
such that for all
n>n_{0}
, the choice of
p_{n}
in
(
(LLR-9)
) satisfies
cn^{4/5}\leq p_{n}(\tilde{h})\leq Cn^{4/5}
for all
\tilde{h}\in[C_{h}^{-1}n^{-1/5}\vee\frac{1}{2n},C_{h}n^{-1/5}]
.

SM3.2
(1–3) is nearly the same as Assumption (LP) in

Tsybakov 2008e
. The only difference is that
SM3.2
(1)
requires the lower bound
\lambda_{0}
to hold uniformly over a range of bandwidth choices,
relative to LP-1 in
Tsybakov 2008e
, which requires
\lambda_{0}
to hold
for some deterministic sequence
h_{n}
.
This is a mild strengthening of LP-1: Note that if
x_{i}
are drawn from a
Lipschitz-continuous, everywhere-positive density
f(x)
, then for
h\to 0,nh\to\infty
,

B_{nx}(h)\approx\int K(t)u(t)u(t)^{\prime}f(x)\,dt\succeq\int K(t)u(t)u(t)^{\prime}\,dt\left({\min_{x\in[0,1]}f(x)}\right)

where
\succ
denotes the positive-definite matrix order. Thus the minimum eigenvalue of

B_{nx}(h)
should be positive irrespective of
x
and
h
. See, also, Lemma 1.5
in
Tsybakov 2008e
.

SM3.2
(2)–(3) are the same as (LP-2)–(LP-3) in

Tsybakov 2008e
. (2) expects that the design points are sufficiently
spread out, and (3) is satisfied by, say, the Epanechnikov kernel.

Lastly, (4) expects that the average effective sample size is about
s_{n}=nh_{n}\asymp n^{-4/5}.

Again, heuristically, if
x_{i}
are drawn from a Lipschitz and everywhere-positive density

f(x)
, then

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}^{2}(x_{j})\approx n\frac{1}{s_{n}^{2}}h_{n}\cdot\int(u(0)^{\prime}B_{n,x_{j}}^{-1}u(t)K(t))^{2}f(x_{j})\,dt=\frac{1}{s_{n}}\int(u(0)^{\prime}B_{n,x_{j}}^{-1}u(t)K(t))^{2}f(x_{j})\,dt.

Hence the mean reciprocal
p_{n}
is of order
s_{n}
. We also remark that

SM3.2
is satisfied by regular design points
x_{i}=i/n
.

Assumption SM3.3
.

The kernel satisfies the following VC subgraph-type conditions. Let

\mathcal{F}_{k}=\left\{{y\mapsto\left({\frac{y-x}{h}}\right)^{k-1}K\left({\frac{y-x}{h}}\right):x\in[0,1],h\in H_{n}}\right\}

for
k=1,2
. For any finitely supported
measure
Q
,

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{F}_{k},L_{2}(Q))\leq C_{K}(1/\epsilon)^{V_{K}}

for
C_{K},V_{K}
that do not depend on
Q
.

SM3.3
is satisfied for a wide range of kernels, e.g. the Epanechnikov
kernel. By Lemma 7.22 in
Sen 2018b
, reproduced as
Lemma

SM3.2
below,
so long as the function
t\mapsto t^{k-1}K(t)
is bounded (assumed in

SM3.2
(3)) and of bounded variation (satisfied by any absolutely continuous
kernel function), the covering number conditions hold by exploiting the finite VC
dimension of subgraphs of these functions.

We now state and prove the main results in this section.
The key to these arguments is
SM3.1
on the bias and variance of local
linear regression estimators.
SM3.1
is uniform in both the evaluation point
x

and the bandwidth
h
, as long as the latter converges at the optimal rate.

Theorem SM3.1
.

Suppose the conditional distribution
\theta_{i}\mid\sigma_{i}
and the design points

\sigma_{1:n}
satisfy
3
,
2
, and
SM3.2
. Moreover,
suppose
m_{0},s_{0}
satisfies
4
(1) with
p=2
. Suppose the kernel

K(\cdot)
satisfies
SM3.3
. Let
\hat{m},\hat{s}
denote the
estimators computed by
Item

(LLR-1)
through
Item

(LLR-11)
. Then,
there exists some
n_{0}>0
such that

(1)

\operatorname{P}\left({\hat{m},\hat{s}\in C_{A_{3}}^{2}([0,1])}\right)=1

(2)

For some
C
depending only on the parameters in the assumptions, for all
n\geq 7
and
t>1
,

\operatorname{P}\left({\max\left({\lVert\hat{m}-m_{0}\rVert_{\infty},\lVert\hat{s}-s_{0}\rVert_{\infty}}\right)\geq Ctn^{-\frac{2}{5}}(\log n)^{1+2/\alpha}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{n^{10}t^{2}}.

(3)

For some
c>0
depending only on the parameters in the assumptions, for all

n\geq n_{0}
,

\operatorname{P}\left({\frac{c}{n}\leq\hat{s}}\right)=1.

Proof.

The first claim is true automatically by the projection to the Hölder space.

The third
claim is true for all
n>n_{0}
automatically by
Item

(LLR-11)
, since
p_{n}\geq cn^{4/5}

and
n^{-4/5}\gtrsim n^{-1}
. For
n\leq n_{0}
, note that
\sum_{i}\ell_{i}(x,h)=\sum_{i}\ell_{i}(x,h)\underbrace{u((x_{i}-x)/h)^{\prime}u(0)}_{1}=\lVert u(0)\rVert^{2}=1

for all
h,x
. Hence

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\ell_{i}^{2}(x,h)\geq\left({\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\ell_{i}(x,h)}\right)^{2}\implies\sum_{i}\ell_{i}^{2}(x,h)\geq\frac{1}{n}\implies p_{n}\leq n

regardless of
h
. As a result, the truncation point for
\hat{s}^{2}
is at least of order

\frac{1}{n}
. This is sufficient for
\hat{s}\geq c/n
.

Now, we show the second claim. Since we assume that
m_{0},s_{0}
lies in the Hölder space
with
s_{0}>s_{0\ell}
, then
projection to the Hölder space (and truncation by
2/(2+p_{n})\min_{i}\sigma_{i}^{2})
worsens
performance by at most a factor of two for all sufficiently large
n
. The projection to
the Hölder space ensures that
\lVert\hat{\eta}-\eta_{0}\rVert_{\infty}
is bounded a.s. for all

n
, so that
we can remove “for all sufficiently large
n
” at the cost of enlarging a constant so
as to accommodate the first finitely many values of
n
.
As a result, it suffices to show that

\operatorname{P}\left({\max\left({\lVert\hat{m}_{\mathrm{raw}}-m_{0}\rVert_{\infty},\lVert\hat{s}_{\mathrm{raw}}-s_{0}\rVert_{\infty}}\right)>Ctn^{-2/5}(\log n)^{\beta}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{n^{10}t^{2}}

for some
C
and
\beta=1+2/\alpha
.

Let
Y_{i}=m_{0}(x_{i})+\xi_{i}
where
\xi_{i}=\theta_{i}-m_{0}(x_{i})+(Y_{i}-\theta_{i})
. Note
that we have simultaneous moment control for
\xi_{i}
:

\max_{i}\mathbb{E}[|\xi_{i}|^{p}]^{1/p}\lesssim p^{1/\alpha}

where
\alpha
is the constant in
2
. Therefore, we can apply

SM3.1
to obtain

\operatorname{P}\left({\lVert\hat{m}_{\mathrm{raw}}-m_{0}\rVert_{\infty}>Ctn^{-2/5}(\log n)^{1+1/\alpha}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2n^{10}t^{2}}

for the local linear regression estimator
\hat{m}_{\mathrm{raw}}
.

The same argument to control
\lVert\hat{s}_{\mathrm{raw}}-s_{0}\rVert_{\infty}
is more involved. First
observe that

|\hat{s}^{2}_{\mathrm{raw}}-s_{0}^{2}|=|\hat{s}_{\mathrm{raw}}-s_{0}|(\hat{s}_{\mathrm{raw}}+s_{0})\geq s_{0\ell}|\hat{s}_{\mathrm{raw}}-s_{0}|.

Also observe that for a positive
f_{0}
,

|\hat{f}\vee g-f_{0}|\leq|\hat{f}-f_{0}|\vee|g|.

As a result, it suffices to control the upper bound in

(
v_{0}(x)\equiv\operatorname{Var}(Y_{i}\mid x_{i}=x)
)

\displaystyle\lVert\hat{s}_{\mathrm{raw}}-s_{0}\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{s_{0\ell}}\left({\lVert\hat{v}-v_{0}\rVert_{\infty}\vee\left({\frac{2}{2+p_{n}}\hat{v}}\right)}\right)

(
SM3.2
)

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert\hat{v}-v_{0}\rVert_{\infty}\vee\frac{\lVert\hat{v}-v_{0}\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert v_{0}\rVert_{\infty}}{2+n^{4/5}}

(SM3.4)

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert\hat{v}-v_{0}\rVert_{\infty}

Now, observe that
\hat{R}_{i}^{2}=R_{i}^{2}+(m_{0}-\hat{m})^{2}-2(m_{0}-\hat{m})\xi_{i}
. Hence,

\displaystyle|\hat{v}(x)-v_{0}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}(x,\hat{h}_{n,s})R_{i}^{2}-v_{0}(x)\right|+\left\{{\lVert m_{0}-\hat{m}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+2\lVert m_{0}-\hat{m}\rVert_{\infty}\left({\max_{i\in[n]}|\xi_{i}|}\right)}\right\}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{i}(x,\hat{h}_{n,s})|

(SM3.5)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}(x,\hat{h}_{n,s})R_{i}^{2}-v_{0}(x)\right|+C\left\{{\lVert m_{0}-\hat{m}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+2\lVert m_{0}-\hat{m}\rVert_{\infty}\left({\max_{i\in[n]}|\xi_{i}|}\right)}\right\}.

By Lemma 1.3 in
Tsybakov 2008e
, the term
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\ell_{i}(x,\hat{h}_{n,s})|
is bounded uniformly in
h
and
x
by a constant. Note that

\tilde{\xi}_{i}\equiv R_{i}^{2}-v_{0}(x_{i})

has simultaneous moment control with a different parameter (
\tilde{\alpha}=\alpha/2
):

\max_{i}(\mathbb{E}|\tilde{\xi}_{i}|^{p})^{1/p}\lesssim p^{2/\alpha}.

Thus, applying
SM3.1
and taking care to plug in
\tilde{\xi},\tilde{\alpha}
, we can bound the first term in (
SM3.5
)

\operatorname{P}\left({\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}(x,\hat{h}_{n,s})R_{i}^{2}-v_{0}(x)\right\rVert_{\infty}\geq Ctn^{-2/5}(\log n)^{1+2/\alpha}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{4n^{10}t^{2}}.

Note that by an application of
Lemma

OA1.7
, for any
a,b>0
, we have
that

\operatorname{P}\left({\max_{i}|\xi_{i}|>C(a,b)t(\log n)^{1/\alpha}}\right)<an^{-b}e^{-t^{2}}

As a result, the second term in (
SM3.5
) admits

\operatorname{P}\left({\lVert m_{0}-\hat{m}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}+2\lVert m_{0}-\hat{m}\rVert_{\infty}\left({\max_{i\in[n]}|\xi_{i}|}\right)>Ctn^{-2/5}(\log n)^{1+2/\alpha}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{4n^{10}t^{2}}

Finally, putting these bounds together, we have that

\operatorname{P}\left({\lVert\hat{v}-v_{0}\rVert_{\infty}>Ctn^{-2/5}(\log n)^{1+2/\alpha}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2n^{10}t^{2}},

where the same bound (with a different constant) holds for
\hat{s}_{\mathrm{raw}}
by
(
SM3.4
).

Combining the bounds for
\hat{m}
and
\hat{s}
, we obtain (
2
).
This concludes the proof.
∎

Theorem SM3.2
.

Under the assumptions of
Theorem

SM3.1
, let
\hat{\eta}=(\hat{m},\hat{s})
denote
estimators computed by
Item

(LLR-1)
through
Item

(LLR-11)
. Then,

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{MSERegret}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta})}\right]\lesssim n^{-2/5}(\log n)^{1+2/\alpha}.

Proof.

Recall the event
A_{n}
in (
A.1
) for
\Delta_{n}=C_{1}n^{-2/5}(\log n)^{\beta}
and
M_{n}=C_{2}(\log n)^{1/\alpha}
, where
C_{1},C_{2}
are to be chosen and

\beta=1+2/\alpha
. Define
\tilde{A}_{n}=A_{n}\cap\left\{{s_{0\ell}/2\leq\hat{s}\leq 2s_{0u}}\right\}
.
Decompose

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{MSERegret}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta})}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{MSERegret}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta})\mathbbm{1}(\tilde{A}_{n})}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{MSERegret}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta})\mathbbm{1}(\tilde{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{C}})}\right].

Note that, for all sufficiently large
n>N
, such that
N
depends only on
C_{1},\beta,s_{\ell},s_{u}
, the event
A_{n}
already implies
\left\{{s_{0\ell}/2\leq\hat{s}\leq 2s_{0u}}\right\}

and hence

A_{n}=\tilde{A}_{n}
. Thus, by

Theorem

SM3.1
, for all sufficiently large
n
, on the event
A_{n}
,
statements analogous to
4
(2–4) hold for the
estimator
\hat{\eta}
. As a result, we may apply
Theorem

A.1
,
mutatis
mutandis
, to obtain that

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{MSERegret}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta})\mathbbm{1}(\tilde{A}_{n})}\right]\lesssim n^{-4/5}(\log n)^{\frac{2+\alpha}{\alpha}+3+2\beta}

for all sufficiently large choices of
C_{1},C_{2}
.

To control
\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{MSERegret}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta})\mathbbm{1}(\tilde{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{C}})}\right]
, we observe that under

Lemma

OA1.6
and
Theorem

SM3.1
(1 and 3), we have that almost
surely on
A_{n}^{\mathrm{C}}
,

\mathrm{MSERegret}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta})\lesssim n^{2}\overline{Z}_{n}^{2}.

Hence, by Cauchy–Schwarz as in
Lemma

OA1.2
,

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{MSERegret}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta})\mathbbm{1}(\tilde{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{C}})}\right]\lesssim\operatorname{P}(\tilde{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{C}})^{1/2}n^{4}(\log n)^{2/\alpha},

where we apply
Lemma

OA1.7
to bound
\mathbb{E}[\overline{Z}_{n}^{4}]
. This bound holds for
all
n\geq 7
.

For all sufficiently large
n>N
,

\operatorname{P}(A_{n}^{\mathrm{C}})=\operatorname{P}(\tilde{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{C}})\leq\operatorname{P}(\overline{Z}_{n}>M_{n})+\operatorname{P}(\lVert\hat{\eta}-\eta_{0}\rVert_{\infty}>\Delta_{n}).

Sufficiently large
C_{1},C_{2}
can be chosen such that the right-hand side is bounded by

n^{-10}
. To wit, we can apply
Theorem

SM3.1
to bound
\lVert\hat{\eta}-\eta_{0}\rVert_{\infty}
. We can apply
Lemma

OA1.7
to bound
\operatorname{P}(\overline{Z}_{n}>M_{n})
.
As a result, we would obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathrm{MSERegret}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta})\mathbbm{1}(\tilde{A}_{n}^{\mathrm{C}})}\right]\lesssim\frac{1}{n}(\log n)^{2/\alpha}

for all sufficiently large
n
.

Since
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{MSERegret}_{n}(\hat{G}_{n},\hat{\eta})]\lesssim n^{4}(\log n)^{2/\alpha}
is finite for all

n
, at the cost of enlarging the implicit constant, we have the result of the theorem
holding for all
n
.
∎

SM3.1.
Auxiliary lemmas

Proposition SM3.1
.

Consider the local linear regression of data
Y_{i}=f_{0}(x_{i})+\xi_{i}
on the design points

x_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
. Suppose
f_{0}
belongs to a Hölder class of order two:

f_{0}\in C_{L}^{2}([0,1])
for some
L>0
. Suppose that the design points satisfy

SM3.2
and the (possibly data-driven) bandwidths
h_{n}
satisfy

SM3.1
. Assume the kernel additionally satisfies
SM3.3
.

Assume that the residuals
\xi_{i}
are mean zero, and there exists a constant
A_{\xi}>0,\alpha>0
such that

\max_{i=1,\ldots,n}(\mathbb{E}[|\xi_{i}|^{p}])^{1/p}\leq A_{\xi}p^{1/\alpha}

for all
p\geq 2
. Let
\ell_{i}(x,h)
be the weights corresponding to local linear
regression, and define the bias part
b(x,h_{n})=\left({\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}(x,h_{n})f_{0}(x_{i})}\right)-f_{0}(x_{i})

and the stochastic part
v(x,h)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}(x,h)\xi_{i}
. Recall that
H_{n}
is the interval for
h_{n}
in
SM3.1
.
Then:

(1)

The bias term is of order
n^{-2/5}
:

\sup_{x\in[0,1],h\in H_{n}}|b(x,h)|\lesssim n^{-2/5}.

(2)

The variance term admits the following large-deviation inequality: For any

a,b>0
, there exists
a constant
C(a,b)
, which may additionally depend on the constants in the assumptions,
such that for all
n>1
and
t\geq 1

\operatorname{P}\left({\sup_{x\in[0,1],h\in H_{n}}|v(x,h)|>C(a,b)\cdot t\cdot(\log n)^{1+1/\alpha}n^{-2/5}}\right)\leq an^{-b}\frac{1}{t^{2}}.

(3)

As a result, let
\hat{f}(\cdot)=b(\cdot,h_{n})+v(\cdot,h_{n})+f_{0}(\cdot)
, we
have that for
any
a,b>0
, there exists a constant
C(a,b)
such that for all
n>1
and
t\geq 1
,

\operatorname{P}\left({\lVert\hat{f}-f_{0}\rVert_{\infty}>C(a,b)t(\log n)^{1+1/\alpha}n^{-2/5}}\right)\leq an^{-b}\frac{1}{t^{2}}.

Proof.

Note that (3) follows immediately from (1) and (2) since the bounds in (1) and (2) are
uniform over all
h\in H_{n}
. We now verify (1) and (2).

(1)

This claim follows immediately from the bound for
b(x_{0})
in Proposition 1.13 in

Tsybakov 2008e
. The argument in
Tsybakov 2008e
shows
that

\sup_{x\in[0,1]}|b(x,h_{n})|\leq Ch_{n}^{2},

which is uniformly bounded by
Cn^{-2/5}
by
SM3.1
. Hence

\sup_{x\in[0,1],h\in H_{n}}|b(x,h)|\lesssim n^{-2/5}.

(2)

Let
M
be a truncation point to be defined. Let

\xi_{i,<M}=\xi_{i}\mathbbm{1}(|\xi_{i}|\leq M)-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{i}\mathbbm{1}(|\xi_{i}|\leq M)]\hskip 9.24994pt\xi_{i,>M}=\xi_{i}\mathbbm{1}(|\xi_{i}|>M)-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{i}\mathbbm{1}(|\xi_{i}|>M)]

be truncated and demeaned variables. Note that

\xi_{i}=\xi_{i,<M}+\xi_{i,>M}.

First, let
V_{1n}(x,h_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}(x,h_{n})\xi_{i,>M}
. Note that by
Cauchy–Schwarz, uniformly over
x,h_{n}
,

\displaystyle V_{1n}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}(x,h_{n})^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i,>M}^{2}

(Lemma 1.3(i) in

Tsybakov 2008e
shows that
|\ell_{i}(x,h_{n})|\leq\frac{C}{nh_{n}}
)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{h_{n}^{2}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i,>M}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{2/5}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i,>M}^{2}

Now, for some
C
related to the implicit constant in the above display,

\operatorname{P}\left({\sup_{x\in[0,1],h_{n}\in H_{n}}V_{1n}^{2}(x,h_{n})>Ct^{2}}\right)\leq\operatorname{P}\left({\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i,>M}^{2}>t^{2}n^{-2/5}}\right)\leq\frac{\max_{i}\mathbb{E}\xi_{i,>M}^{2}}{t^{2}}n^{2/5}.

We note that by Cauchy–Schwarz,

\mathbb{E}[\xi_{i,>M}^{2}]\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\xi_{i}^{4}]}\sqrt{\operatorname{P}(|\xi_{i}|>M)}\lesssim\sqrt{\operatorname{P}(|\xi_{i}|>M)}\leq\exp\left({-cM^{\alpha}}\right)

where
c
depends on
A_{\xi}
.
Hence, for a potentially different constant
C
,

\operatorname{P}\left({\sup_{x\in[0,1],h_{n}\in H_{n}}|V_{1n}(x,h_{n})|>Ct}\right)\leq\exp\left({-cM^{\alpha}-2\log t+\frac{2}{5}\log n}\right).

Next, consider the process

\displaystyle V_{2n}(x,h_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell_{i}(x,h_{n})\xi_{i,<M}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nh_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{u(0)^{\prime}B_{nx}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}}_{A_{1}(x,h_{n})}K\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)\xi_{i,<M}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\frac{1}{nh_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{u(0)^{\prime}B_{nx}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}}_{A_{2}(x,h_{n})}K\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)\xi_{i,<M}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{A_{1}(x,h_{n})}{h_{n}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)\xi_{i,<M}+\frac{A_{2}(x,h_{n})}{h_{n}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)\xi_{i,<M}.

Note that, by
SM3.2
(1), uniformly over
x\in[0,1]
and
h_{n}\in H_{n}
,

|A_{k}(x,h_{n})|\leq\lVert u(0)^{\prime}B_{nx}^{-1}\rVert\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{0}}.

By triangle inequality,

\displaystyle V_{2n}(x,h_{n})

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{h_{n}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)\xi_{i,<M}\right|+\frac{1}{h_{n}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)\left({\frac{x_{i}-x}{h_{n}}}\right)\xi_{i,<M}\right|

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}h_{n}}V_{2n,1}(x,h_{n})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}h_{n}}V_{2n,2}(x,h_{n}).

We will aim to control the
\psi_{2}
-norm of the left-hand side. Note that it suffices to
control the
\psi_{2}
-norm of both terms on the right-hand side:

\left\lVert\sup_{x\in[0,1],h_{n}\in H_{n}}|V_{2n}(x,h_{n})|\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}h_{n}}\max_{k=1,2}\left({\left\lVert\sup_{x\in[0,1],h_{n}\in H_{n}}|V_{2n,k}(x,h_{n})|\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}}\right).

The above display follows from replacing the sum with two times the maximum and Lemma
2.2.2 in
van der Vaart & Wellner 1996e
.

We will do so by applying
Lemma

SM3.1
. The analogue of
f
in
Lemma

SM3.1

is

t\mapsto f(t;x,h)=\left({\frac{t-x}{h}}\right)^{k-1}K\left({\frac{t-x}{h}}\right)

for
V_{2n,k},k=1,2
. Naturally, the analogues of
\mathcal{F}
is

\mathcal{F}_{k}=\left\{{t\mapsto f(t;x,h):x\in[0,1],h\in H_{n}}\right\}\cup\{t\mapsto 0\}.

Note that

f(t;x,h)\leq\mathbbm{1}(|t-x|\leq h)K_{0}

and thus the diameter of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is at most

\sup_{A\subset[0,1]:\lambda(A)\leq 4C_{h}n^{-1/5}}K_{0}\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}(x_{i}\in A)}\lesssim n^{-1/10}

by
SM3.2
(2). Therefore, by
SM3.3
, we apply

Lemma

SM3.1
and obtain that for
k=1,2

\left\lVert\sup_{x\in[0,1],h\in H_{n}}|V_{2n,k}(x,h)|\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\lesssim Mn^{-1/10}\sqrt{\log n}.

Finally, this argument shows that

\left\lVert\sup_{x\in[0,1],h\in H_{n}}|V_{2n}(x,h)|\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}h_{n}n^{1/10}}M\sqrt{\log n}\lesssim n^{-2/5}M\sqrt{\log n}.

Putting things together, we can choose
M=(c_{m}\log n)^{1/\alpha}
for sufficiently
large
c_{m}
so that by (
2
),

\operatorname{P}\left({\sup_{x\in[0,1],h\in H_{n}}|V_{1n}(x,h)|>Ctn^{-2/5}}\right)\leq\frac{a}{2}n^{-b}\frac{1}{t^{2}},

where
c_{m}
depends on
a,b
.
The bound (
2
) in turns shows that

\operatorname{P}\left({\sup_{x\in[0,1],h_{n}\in H_{n}}|V_{2n}(x,h_{n})|>C(a,b)t(\log n)^{\frac{2+\alpha}{2\alpha}}n^{-2/5}}\right)\leq 2e^{-t^{2}}

Taking
t=\sqrt{b\log n+\log(a/4)}s
gives

\operatorname{P}\left({\sup_{x\in[0,1],h_{n}\in H_{n}}|V_{2n}(x,h_{n})|>C(a,b)s(\log n)^{1+1/\alpha}n^{-2/5}e^{-s^{2}}}\right)\leq\frac{a}{2}n^{-b}e^{-s^{2}}<\frac{a}{2}n^{-b}\frac{1}{s^{2}}

for all
s>1
.

Therefore, combining the two bounds,

\operatorname{P}\left({\sup_{x\in[0,1],h_{n}\in H_{n}}|v(x,h_{x})|>C(a,b)t(\log n)^{1+1/\alpha}n^{-2/5}}\right)\leq an^{-b}\frac{1}{t^{2}}.

∎

Lemma SM3.1
.

Suppose
\xi_{i}
are bounded by
M\geq 1
and mean zero. Consider the process

V_{n}(f)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})\xi_{i}

over a class of real-valued functions
f\in\mathcal{F}
and evaluation points

x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in[0,1]
.
Define the seminorm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{n}

relative to
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
by

\lVert f\rVert_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})^{2}.

Suppose
0\in\mathcal{F}
and

\mathcal{F}
has
polynomial covering numbers:

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{n})\leq C(1/\epsilon)^{V}\hskip 9.24994pt\epsilon\in[0,1]

where
C,V>0
depend solely on
\mathcal{F}
.
Then

\left\lVert\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}|V_{n}(f)|\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\lesssim M\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{F})\sqrt{\log(1/\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{F}))},

where
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{F})=\sup_{f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{F}}\lVert f_{1}-f_{2}\rVert_{n}
.

Proof.

The process
V_{n}(f)
has subgaussian increments with respect to
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{n}
:

\lVert V_{n}(f_{1})-V_{n}(f_{2})\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\lesssim M\lVert f_{1}-f_{2}\rVert_{n}.

Hence, by Dudley’s chaining argument (e.g. Corollary 2.2.5 in
van der Vaart & Wellner 1996e
), for
some fixed
f_{0}\in\mathcal{F}
,

\left\lVert\sup_{f}V_{n}(f)\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\leq\lVert V_{n}(f_{0})\rVert_{\psi_{2}}+CM\int_{0}^{\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{F})}\sqrt{\log N(\delta,\mathcal{F},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{n})}\,d\delta.

Note that (i) the metric entropy
integral is bounded by
C\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{F})\sqrt{\log(1/\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{F}))}
, and (ii) for a fixed
f_{0}
,
\lVert V_{n}(f_{0})\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\lesssim\lVert f_{0}\rVert_{n}M\leq\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{F})M
since
0\in\mathcal{F}
. Therefore,

\left\lVert\sup_{f}V_{n}(f)\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\lesssim M\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{F})\sqrt{\log(1/\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{F}))}.

∎

Lemma SM3.2

(Lemma 7.22(ii) in
Sen 2018b
)
.

Let
q(\cdot)
be a real-valued function of bounded variation on

\mathbb{R}
. The covering number of
\mathcal{F}=\left\{{x\mapsto q(ax+b):(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}}\right\}

satisfies

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{2}(Q))\leq K_{1}\epsilon^{-V_{1}}

for some
K_{1}
and
V_{1}
and for a constant envelope.

Appendix SM4
Auxiliary lemmas for
Theorems

2
and
3

Lemma SM4.1
.

In the proof of
Theorem

2
, suppose
Y_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{0}(\sigma_{i}),s_{0}^{2}+\sigma_{i}^{2})
, then

\inf_{\hat{m}}\sup_{m_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{m}(\sigma_{i})-m_{0}(\sigma_{i}))^{2}}\right]\gtrsim_{\mathcal{H}}n^{-2p/(2p+1)},

where the infimum is over all
estimators of
m_{0}
from
(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
and the supremum is over the Hölder
space
m_{0}\in C_{A_{1}}^{p}([\sigma_{\ell},\sigma_{u}])
.

Proof.

First, note that learning
m_{0}
from
(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})
is a nonparametric
regression problem with heteroskedastic variances. This problem is more difficult than
a corresponding problem with homoskedastic variances
\sigma_{\ell}^{2}+s_{0}^{2}
, since we may
represent

Y_{i}=\theta_{i}+\sigma_{\ell}W_{i}+(\sigma_{i}^{2}-\sigma_{\ell}^{2})^{1/2}U_{i}

for independent Gaussians
W_{i},U_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Let
V_{i}=\theta_{i}+\sigma_{\ell}W_{i}
.
Note that we can do no worse for estimating
m_{0}
with
(V_{i},\sigma_{i})
than with
(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})
, and estimating
m_{0}
from
(V_{i},\sigma_{i})
is a homoskedastic regression
problem, where
V_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{0}(\sigma_{i}),\sigma_{\ell}^{2}+s_{0}^{2})
. It remains to show that
the minimax rate for estimating
m_{0}
on the grid points
\sigma_{1:n}
from
(V_{i},\sigma_{i})
is
n^{-2p/(2p+1)}
.

Since we simply have a nonparametric regression problem,
we may translate and rescale so that the design points
\sigma_{1:n}
are equally spaced
in
[0,1]
(
\sigma_{i}=i/n
) and the variance of
V_{i}
is 1—potentially changing the
constant
A_{1}
for
the Hölder smoothness condition. Corollary 2.3 in
Tsybakov 2008e
shows
a lower bound for integrated MSE:

\inf_{\tilde{m}}\sup_{m_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\int_{0}^{1}(\tilde{m}(x)-m_{0}(x))^{2}\,dx}\right]\gtrsim_{\mathcal{H}}n^{-\frac{2p}{2p+1}}

where the infimum is over all (randomized) estimators using
(V_{i},\sigma_{i})
. It thus
suffices to connect the MSE objective over the fixed design points

\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}
to the integrated
MSE.

Observe that for any
\hat{m}(\sigma_{1}),\ldots,\hat{m}(\sigma_{n})
, we can define the piecewise constant function
\tilde{m}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
such
that it is equal to
\hat{m}(\sigma_{i})
on
[(i-1)/n,i/n)
. Now, note that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}(\tilde{m}(x)-m_{0}(x))^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{[(i-1)/n,i/n]}(\tilde{m}(x)-m_{0}(x))^{2}\,dx

(
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{[(i-1)/n,i/n]}(\tilde{m}(x)-m_{0}(\sigma_{i}))^{2}+(m_{0}(\sigma_{i})-m_{0}(x))^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[{\frac{1}{n}(\hat{m}_{i}-m_{0}(\sigma_{i}))^{2}+\frac{L^{2}}{n^{3}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{m}_{i}-m_{0}(\sigma_{i}))^{2}+\frac{2L^{2}}{n^{2}}.

The third line follows by observing that
m_{0}(\cdot)
is Lipschitz for some constant
L

that depends solely on
p,A_{1}
, since
p\geq 1
in
4
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{m}}\sup_{m_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{m}(\sigma_{i})-m_{0}(\sigma_{i}))^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\inf_{\tilde{m}}\sup_{m_{0}}\left\{{\mathbb{E}\left[{\int_{0}^{1}(\tilde{m}(x)-m_{0}(x))^{2}\,dx}\right]-\frac{2L^{2}}{n^{2}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\gtrsim_{\mathcal{H}}n^{-\frac{2p}{2p+1}}.\qed

Lemma SM4.2
.

Assume that
\theta_{i}\mid\sigma_{i}
has mean
m_{0}(\sigma)
and variance
s_{0}^{2}(\sigma)
, without
assuming (
2.4
). Consider the decision rule
(
2.4.1
) and denote it by
\delta^{*}
.
Then

\delta^{*}(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})\in\argmin_{\delta(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})\in L}\mathbb{E}\left[{(\delta(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})-\theta_{i})^{2}\mid\sigma_{i}}\right]

where
L=\left\{{\delta(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})=a(\sigma_{i})+b(\sigma_{i})Y_{i}:a(\cdot),b(\cdot)\text{
measurable}}\right\}.

Proof.

For a given
a(\cdot),b(\cdot)
, we can compute by bias-variance decomposition,

\mathbb{E}[(\delta(Y_{i},\sigma_{i})-\theta_{i})^{2}\mid\sigma_{i}]=(a(\sigma_{i})+b(\sigma_{i})m(\sigma_{i})-m(\sigma_{i}))^{2}+b^{2}(\sigma_{0})s_{0}^{2}+b^{2}(\sigma_{0})\sigma_{i}^{2}+\sigma_{i}^{2}-2b(\sigma_{i})s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i}).

Minimizing the above expression for
a(\sigma_{i}),b(\sigma_{i})
yields

b(\sigma_{i})=\frac{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})}{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})+\sigma_{i}^{2}}\hskip 9.24994pta(\sigma_{i})=(1-b(\sigma_{i}))m(\sigma_{i}).

This corresponds exactly to the decision rule
\delta^{*}
.
∎

Lemma SM4.3
.

Consider the setup of
Theorem

3
. The minimax risk is achieved by the
decision rule (
2.4.1
). That is, let
\delta_{i}^{*}
denote the decision
rule
(
2.4.1
). Then,

\sup_{P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(m_{0},s_{0})}\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\delta_{i}^{*}-\theta_{i})^{2}}\right]=\inf_{\delta_{1:n}}\sup_{P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(m_{0},s_{0})}\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\delta_{i}-\theta_{i})^{2}}\right]

where the infimum is taken over all (randomized) decision rules
\delta_{i}(Y_{1:n},\sigma_{1:n})
, with knowledge of
m_{0},s_{0}
.

Proof.

The
\geq
direction is immediate. We consider the
\leq
direction.
Note that

\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\delta_{i}^{*}-\theta_{i})^{2}}\right]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})\sigma_{i}^{2}}{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})+\sigma_{i}^{2}}

for all
P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(m_{0},s_{0})
, regardless of
P_{0}
. Thus,

\sup_{P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(m_{0},s_{0})}\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\delta_{i}^{*}-\theta_{i})^{2}}\right]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})\sigma_{i}^{2}}{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})+\sigma_{i}^{2}}.

Suppose
P_{0}
denotes the distribution where
\theta_{i}\mid\sigma_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{0}(\sigma_{i}),s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i}))
. Then, for any decision rule
\delta_{i}
,

\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\delta_{i}-\theta_{i})^{2}}\right]\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})\sigma_{i}^{2}}{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})+\sigma_{i}^{2}}.

This is because the right-hand side is the Bayes risk under the Gaussian model. As a
result,

\inf_{\delta_{1:n}}\sup_{P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(m_{0},s_{0})}\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\delta_{i}-\theta_{i})^{2}}\right]\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})\sigma_{i}^{2}}{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})+\sigma_{i}^{2}}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma SM4.4
.

Given
s_{0}(\cdot),m_{0}(\cdot)
, let

s_{0}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})+(m_{0}(\sigma_{i})-m_{0})^{2})\text{ and }m_{0}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{0}(\sigma_{i}).

Fix
C>0
, there exists choices of
s_{0}(\cdot)>0,\sigma_{i},m_{0}(\cdot),P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(m_{0},s_{0})
such that
\max_{i}s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})/\sigma_{i}^{2}<C
but

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\hat{\theta}_{i}-\theta_{i})^{2}}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{P_{0}}\left[{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\check{\theta}_{i}-\theta_{i})^{2}}\right]}

is arbitrarily large, where
\hat{\theta}_{i}
are the
independent-gauss
posterior means and

\check{\theta}_{i}
are the
close
-
gauss
posterior means:

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{s_{0}^{2}+\sigma_{i}^{2}}m_{0}+\frac{s_{0}^{2}}{s_{0}^{2}+\sigma_{i}^{2}}Y_{i}

\displaystyle\check{\theta}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})+\sigma_{i}^{2}}m_{0}(\sigma_{i})+\frac{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})}{s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})+\sigma_{i}^{2}}Y_{i}.

Proof.

Choose
\sigma_{i}=1+i/n
. Choose a constant
s_{0}(\sigma_{i})=\epsilon>0
and some
non-constant
m_{0}(\sigma_{i})
normalized so that
m_{0}=0
. Thus
s_{0}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{0}(\sigma_{i})^{2}\equiv K
. Thus,

\operatorname{Var}(\hat{\theta}_{i})\geq\left({\frac{K}{K+\sigma_{i}^{2}}}\right)^{2}(\sigma_{i}^{2}+s_{0}^{2}(\sigma^{2}))>c>0

for some
c>0
for all
\epsilon>0
. Therefore, the numerator is bounded below by

c
. The denominator converges to zero as
\epsilon\to 0
. With this choice,
\max_{i}s_{0}^{2}(\sigma_{i})/\sigma_{i}^{2}<\epsilon
is eventually smaller than any positive
C
. Thus
the ratio is arbitrarily large as we take
\epsilon\to 0
.
∎

Appendix SM5
Maximum posterior discrepancy of priors satisfying moment constraints

This section contains the main result of
Chen 2023b
(arXiv:2303.08653) and
supersedes that paper.
45
45

45

I am grateful to Isaiah Andrews, Xiao-Li
Meng, Natesh
Pillai, Neil Shephard, and Elie Tamer for their comments. The previous version of the
paper (arXiv:2303.08653v1) claimed that
R(G_{1},\sigma;G_{0})
is uniformly bounded over
all
G_{0},G_{1},\sigma>0
, subjected to the constraints on the first two moments of

G_{0},G_{1}
. Regrettably, it contained a critical error that rendered its proof incorrect.
In particular, in that version, the display before (A1) on p.4 is incorrect: Posterior
means of mixture priors are mixtures of posterior means under each mixing component, but
the mixing weights are posterior probabilities assigned to each mixing component; thus,
the mixing weights depend on the data rather than being fixed.
This section partially restores that result.
Theorem

SM5.1

shows that the maximum Bayes risk under
G_{0}
is uniformly bounded over all
G_{0},G_{1},\sigma^{2}
where
G_{1}
satisfies an additional tail condition (
SM5
). The
bound we obtain depends on the tail condition, and thus
Theorem

SM5.1
is insufficient
for the result claimed in arXiv:2303.08653v1.
The
notation and setup is entirely self-contained.

Consider an observation
X
whose likelihood is
X\mid\theta\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta,\sigma^{2})
for some known

\sigma^{2}
. There are two priors for
\theta
, denoted by
G_{0}
and
G_{1}
. Suppose both
priors have zero mean and have finite variances bounded by
V>0
. Consider the decision
problem of estimating
\theta
under squared error, with
L(a,\theta)=(a-\theta)^{2}
. For
the Bayesian with prior
G_{1}
, the Bayes decision rule is the posterior mean
\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]
under the prior
G_{1}
. This decision rule attains Bayes risk under the
prior
G_{0}

R(G_{1},\sigma;G_{0})\equiv\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim G_{0}}\left[{(\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]-\theta)^{2}}\right].

We can think of
R(G_{1},\sigma;G_{0})
as a measure of decision quality under
disagreement. It measures the quality of
G_{1}
’s decision from
G_{0}
’s point of view.
When
G_{1}\neq G_{0}
, how large can
R(G_{1},\sigma;G_{0})
be?

Since

R(G_{1},\sigma;G_{0})\leq 2\left({\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}[\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}[\theta^{2}]}\right)\leq 2V+2\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}[\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]^{2}],

it thus suffices to bound
\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}[\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]^{2}]

modulo constants. That is, it suffices to bound the 2-norm
\lVert\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]\rVert^{2}
under the law
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})\star G_{0}
.

The rest of the section shows that this quantity is uniformly bounded over all
G_{0},G_{1},\sigma^{2}>0
. Specifically,
Lemma

SM5.1
shows that for all
G_{0},G_{1}
that are mean zero and have variance bounded by
V
,
\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}[\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]^{2}]
is bounded by a constant that depends only on
(V,\sigma^{2})
. This bound is
large when
\sigma^{2}
is large. To improve this bound,
Theorem

SM5.1
then shows
that, if
G_{1}
additionally satisfies some conditions on its tail behavior,
\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}[\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]^{2}]
is bounded by a constant that depends only on
V
and the tail condition—and
does not depend on
\sigma
.

Lemma SM5.1
.

Suppose
G_{0},G_{1}
have mean zero and variances bounded by
V
, then

\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}[\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]^{2}]\leq 6V+4\sigma^{2}

uniformly over
G_{0},G_{1},\sigma^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
f_{G,\sigma}(x)=\int f_{X}(x\mid\theta)\,G(d\theta)
.

Jiang 2020h
(Lemma 1) shows that

\left({\frac{f^{\prime}_{G,\sigma}(x)}{f_{G,\sigma}(x)}}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\log\left({\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma^{2}f^{2}_{G,\sigma}(x)}}\right).

Plugging in the bound (
SM5.2
) in
Lemma

SM5.2
, we have that
for all
X
,

\left({\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]-X}\right)^{2}=\left({\sigma^{2}\frac{f^{\prime}_{G_{1},\sigma}(x)}{f_{G_{1},\sigma}(x)}}\right)^{2}\leq\sigma^{4}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\frac{X^{2}+V}{\sigma^{2}}=X^{2}+V,

where the first equality is due to Tweedie’s formula.

Now, note that

(\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X])^{2}\leq 2\left({(\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]-X)^{2}+X^{2}}\right).

Hence,

\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}\left[{\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X])^{2}}\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}[2X^{2}+V]\leq 2(2(V+\sigma^{2})+V)=6V+4\sigma^{2}.\qed

To show a more powerful bound, we require a stronger condition on the tails of
G_{1}
and
derive bounds that are independent of
\sigma
but are dependent on the tail conditions.
In particular, assume

\max\left({1-G_{1}(s),G_{1}(-s)}\right)\leq C_{G_{1}}s^{-k}

for some
k>2
and
C_{G_{1}}>0
, for all
s>0
. We will also assume that
\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta^{2}\mid X]
exists almost surely. Note that if
\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}|\theta|^{2+\epsilon}<m
, then
k
can be taken to be
2+\epsilon
and
C_{G_{1}}
can be taken to
be
m
by Markov’s inequality. In the rest of the proof, we let
C_{t}<\infty
denote a
positive constant that depends only on
t
. Occurrences of

C_{t}
might correspond to different constant values.

Theorem SM5.1
.

Suppose
k>2.
There exists a constant
Q<\infty
that depends solely on
(C_{G_{1}},k,V)
such that, uniformly for all
(G_{0},G_{1})
and
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, where (i)
G_{0},G_{1}

have mean zero and variance bounded by
V
and (ii)
G_{1}
satisfies
(
SM5
) with
(C_{G_{1}},k)
,

\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}\left[{\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]^{2}}\right]\leq Q.

Proof.

Assume that
\sigma^{2}\geq 1
. For all
\sigma^{2}<1
, we can apply

Lemma

SM5.1

so that
\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}\left[{\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]^{2}}\right]\leq 6V+4
.

Observe that

(Jensen’s
inequality)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}\left[{\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta\mid X]^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}\left[{\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta^{2}\mid X]}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}\left[{\int_{0}^{\infty}P_{G_{1}}(\theta^{2}>t\mid X)\,dt}\right]

(Change of variable
s=\sqrt{t}
)

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}\left[{\int_{0}^{\infty}sP_{G_{1}}(|\theta|>s\mid X)\,ds}\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}\left[{\int_{0}^{\infty}sP_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X)\,ds+\int_{0}^{\infty}sP_{G_{1}}(-\theta>-s\mid X)\,ds}\right].

Therefore, it suffices to bound the first term, since the second term follows by a
symmetric argument. We do so in the remainder of the proof. Here,
\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta^{2}\mid X]
exists since
(\theta^{2},X)
is integrable.

Writing out the first term as an integral:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}\left[{\int_{0}^{\infty}sP_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X)\,ds}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mu=-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{s=0}^{\infty}sP_{G_{1}}[\theta>s\mid X=x]\,dsf_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dx\,G_{0}(d\mu)

(Fubini’s theorem)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mu=-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{s=0}^{\infty}s\int_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}P_{G_{1}}[\theta>s\mid X=x]f_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dx\,ds\,G_{0}(d\mu).

The outer integral in
\mu
can be decomposed into
|\mu|\leq\sigma
and
|\mu|>\sigma
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}\left[{\int_{0}^{\infty}sP_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X)\,ds}\right]

(SM5.2)

\displaystyle=\int_{|\mu|>\sigma}\int_{s=0}^{\infty}s\int_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}P_{G_{1}}[\theta>s\mid X=x]f_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dx\,ds\,G_{0}(d\mu)

(SM5.3)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int_{|\mu|<\sigma}\int_{s=0}^{\infty}s\int_{x=-\infty}^{\infty}P_{G_{1}}[\theta>s\mid X=x]f_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dx\,ds\,G_{0}(d\mu)

First, we consider (
SM5.2
). Decompose the integral in
x
further along
x\leq s/2
and
x>s/2
:

(SM5.4)

(
SM5.2
)

\displaystyle=\int_{|\mu|>\sigma}\int_{0}^{\infty}s\int_{s/2}^{\infty}P_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X=x)f_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dx\,ds\,G_{0}(d\mu)

(SM5.5)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\int_{|\mu|>\sigma}\int_{0}^{\infty}s\int_{-\infty}^{s/2}P_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X=x)f_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dx\,ds\,G_{0}(d\mu).

For large
\mu
and large
x
(
SM5.4
), we have that

(
P_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X=x)\leq 1
)

(
SM5.4
)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|\mu|>\sigma}\int_{0}^{\infty}s\int_{s/2}^{\infty}f_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dxdsG_{0}(d\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{|\mu|>\sigma}\int_{0}^{\infty}sP(X>s/2\mid\mu)\,ds\,G_{0}(d\mu)

(
\int 2sP(X>s\mid\mu)\,ds\leq\mathbb{E}[X^{2}\mid\mu]
)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{|\mu|>\sigma}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[X^{2}\mid\mu]}_{\mu^{2}+\sigma^{2}\leq 2\mu^{2}}\,G_{0}(d\mu)\leq C\int 2\mu^{2}\,G_{0}(d\mu)\leq C_{V}.

For large
\mu
and small
x
(
SM5.5
), note that for
x<s/2<s
, by

Lemma

SM5.2

(
f_{X}(x\mid\theta)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma}e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(x-s)^{2}}
)

\displaystyle P_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X=x)

\displaystyle\leq C_{V}e^{x^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})}e^{-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(x-s)^{2}}(1-G_{1}(s)).

Now, integrating the above display with respect to
f_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dx
yields

\int_{-\infty}^{s/2}P_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X=x)f_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dx\leq C_{V}(1-G_{1}(s))\cdot\frac{\sigma^{2}}{s}\leq C_{V}(1-G_{1}(s))\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}

Finally, integrating it again with respect to
s
yields

\int_{0}^{\infty}s\times C_{V}(1-G_{1}(s))\frac{\mu^{2}}{s}\,ds=C_{V}\mu^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}1-G_{1}(s)\,ds\leq C_{V}\mu^{2}\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[|\theta|]\leq C_{V}\mu^{2}.

Therefore,

\eqref{eq:term2_}\leq C_{V}\mathbb{E}_{G_{0}}\mu^{2}\leq C_{V}.

This shows that (
SM5.2
) is uniformly bounded.

Moving on to (
SM5.3
), we first decompose the integral on
s
into
s\leq K

and
s>K
, for some
K\geq e
to be chosen:

\eqref{eq:large_sigma}\leq\underbrace{\int_{|\mu|<\sigma}\int_{0}^{K}s\,ds\,G_{0}(d\mu)}_{\leq K^{2}/2}+\int_{|\mu|<\sigma}\int_{K}^{\infty}s\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}P_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X=x)f_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dx\,G_{0}(d\mu)

Thus we focus on the piece where
s>K
. Fix

u=C\sigma\sqrt{\log(s)}

for some

C\geq 2
to be chosen. On
s>K
,

u/\sigma>2
and thus
\frac{u}{\sigma}-1>\frac{u}{2\sigma}
.
Observe that by
Lemma

SM5.2
and the fact that
\sigma>1
,

P_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X=x)\leq C_{V}\exp\left({\frac{x^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}\right)(1-G(s)).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}P_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X=x)f_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dx

(
P_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X=x)\leq 1
)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|x|\leq u}P_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X=x)f_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dx+\operatorname{P}(|X|>u\mid\mu)

(
\overline{\Phi}=1-\Phi
is the
complementary Gaussian CDF)

\displaystyle\leq C_{V}e^{-\mu^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})}(1-G(s))\left[{\frac{\sinh\left({\frac{\mu}{\sigma}\frac{u}{\sigma}}\right)}{\mu/\sigma}}\right]+2\underbrace{\overline{\Phi}\left({\frac{u}{\sigma}-\frac{|\mu|}{\sigma}}\right)}_{\leq\overline{\Phi}\left({u/\sigma-1}\right)\leq\overline{\Phi}\left({\frac{u}{2\sigma}}\right)}

(SM5.8)

\displaystyle\leq C_{V}(1-G(s))\frac{\sinh\left({\frac{\mu}{\sigma}\frac{u}{\sigma}}\right)}{\mu/\sigma}+2\overline{\Phi}\left({\frac{u}{2\sigma}}\right)

where the second inequality follows from directly integrating the upper bound
(
SM5
) within
|x|\leq u
. Now, observe that, for
|c|<1
and
t>0
,

(
\sinh(x)/x
is an even
function)

\displaystyle t\frac{\sinh(ct)}{ct}

\displaystyle\leq t\frac{\sinh(|c|t)}{|c|t}

(
\sinh(x)/x
is an increasing function on
x>0
)

\displaystyle\leq t\frac{\sinh(t)}{t}

(
\sinh(x)=(e^{x}-e^{-x})/2\leq\frac{1}{2}e^{x}
for
x>0
)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}e^{t}.

Therefore,

(
SM5.8
)

\displaystyle\leq C_{V}(1-G(s))\exp\left({\frac{C}{\sqrt{\log s}}\log s}\right)+2\overline{\Phi}(C\sqrt{\log s})

(
Lemma

SM5.3
)

\displaystyle\leq C_{V}(1-G(s))\exp\left({\frac{C}{\sqrt{\log s}}\log s}\right)+\exp\left({-\frac{C^{2}}{2}\log s}\right).

Choose
C=k
and
K=\exp\left({1\vee\frac{(2C)^{2}}{(k-2)^{2}}}\right)
. This yields that, for

s>K
,

\frac{C}{\sqrt{\log s}}\leq\frac{k-2}{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{C^{2}}{2}=\frac{k^{2}}{2}.

Hence, integrating with respect to
s
:

\displaystyle\int_{K}^{\infty}s\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}P_{G_{1}}(\theta>s\mid X=x)f_{X}(x\mid\mu)\,dx\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\int_{K}^{\infty}s\left({C_{V}(1-G(s))\exp\left({\frac{k-2}{2}\log s}\right)+\exp\left({-\frac{k^{2}}{2}\log s}\right)}\right)\,ds

\displaystyle\leq C_{V}C_{G_{1}}\int_{K}^{\infty}s^{1-k+\frac{k-2}{2}}\,ds+\int_{K}^{\infty}s^{-k^{2}/2+1}\,ds

(
1-k+(k-2)/2<-1
and
-k^{2}/2+1<-1
)

\displaystyle\leq C_{V}C_{G_{1}}C_{k}+C_{k}.

as both integrals converge and depend only on
k>2
. Returning to
(
SM5
), this shows that (
SM5.3
) is uniformly bounded
with a constant that depends only on
V,C_{G_{1}},k
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma SM5.2
.

Suppose
G_{1}
has mean zero and variance bounded by
V
. Let

f_{G_{1},\sigma}(x)\equiv\int f_{X}(x\mid\theta)\,G_{1}(d\theta).

Then,

f_{G_{1},\sigma}(x)\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma}\exp\left({-\frac{x^{2}+V}{2\sigma^{2}}}\right)\text{, or }\frac{1}{f_{G_{1},\sigma}(x)}\leq\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma\exp\left({\frac{x^{2}+V}{2\sigma^{2}}}\right).

Proof.

Observe that, by Jensen’s inequality,

f_{G_{1},\sigma}(x)\equiv\int f_{X}(x\mid\theta)\,G_{1}(d\theta)\geq\exp\int\log f_{X}(x\mid\theta)\,G_{1}(d\theta).

We compute

\log f_{X}(x\mid\theta)=\log\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma}-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}(x-\theta)^{2}.

Note that
\mathbb{E}_{\theta\sim G_{1}}[(x-\theta)^{2}]=x^{2}-2x\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}[\theta]+\mathbb{E}_{G_{1}}\theta^{2}\leq x^{2}+V
.
Thus (
SM5.2
) follows. ∎

Lemma SM5.3
.

For all
x\geq 0
,
\overline{\Phi}(x)\leq\frac{1}{2}e^{-x^{2}/2}
.

Proof.

Note that
\overline{\Phi}(0)=\frac{1}{2}
and thus the bound holds with equality at
x=0
.
Differentiate,

\overline{\Phi}^{\prime}(x)=-\varphi(x)\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{2}e^{-x^{2}/2}=-\frac{x}{2}e^{-x^{2}/2}=(-x\sqrt{\pi/2})\varphi(x)

For
x\in[0,\sqrt{2/\pi}]
,

\frac{d}{dx}\overline{\Phi}(x)\leq\frac{d}{dx}\frac{1}{2}e^{-x^{2}/2}\implies\overline{\Phi}(x)\leq\frac{1}{2}e^{-x^{2}/2}.

Note that since Mill’s ratio is bounded by
1/x
, we have that for all
x>0

\overline{\Phi}(x)\leq\varphi(x)/x.

Take
x>\sqrt{2/\pi}
, we have that

\overline{\Phi}(x)\leq\varphi(x)\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}=\frac{1}{2}e^{-x^{2}/2}.

Hence the inequality holds for all
x\geq 0
.
∎
